Table 7.3: Polygon datasets used for aggregation of raster data 
For each polygon in each dataset, the raster statistics were gathered as described in Table 7.2, so that we could associate them with polygon attributes described in Table 7.3. This allowed for example square footage of road pavement and non-road pavement to be summarized for residential parcels or the right- of- way in a particular Countywide Statistical Area separately. 
To generate school polygons, we began with a dataset of school location points, including school names, and spatially joined it to the parcel polygons dataset. However, many schools are actually built on multiple parcels, sometimes spanning several city blocks. To address this, we buffered the initial "seed" polygon (where the school point landed) by 100 feet to capture parcels that might lie just across a historical street right-of-way, and included any parcels within that buffer owned by a school district. We then iterated this process to ensure all school parcels in a group would be counted for that school. 
This method has limitations. For example, two adjacent schools in the same district (such as a high school and an elementary school that are within 100 feet of each other) will both include each other’s pavement. <comment_start id=kix.mtsprvj10n3>This method also excludes landcover data from ROW areas that lie between the original parcels where multi-parcel schools were constructed. <comment_end id=kix.mtsprvj10n3>Many schools span  multiple historical parcels that were never combined into one. We tested methods to account for this, such as building a convex hull around groups of parcels, but this leads to overestimating pavement, so we opted to exclude those interparcel regions to take a more conservative approach.
For Vision Zero Road segments, we buffered each 1 mile line by 50 ft, to yield a 1 mile by 100 foot rectangle for each segment. These segments include an area that approximates the “streetscape” of that segment, which includes the adjacent sidewalks, street trees and other landscape features that belong to the human experience of traversing said segments. 
The aggregation of raster-based geospatial data (including temperature, landcover classifications, flood risk categories, dasymetric population and elevation) to polygon features was performed using a suite of custom Python scripts. These scripts leveraged several core libraries: **GeoPandas** for managing vector polygon data, **Rasterio** for raster data input/output and spatial operations, and **NumPy** for numerical computations.
The general workflow for each polygon dataset involved the following key steps:
1.  **Data Ingestion and Preparation:** Input polygon layers (in GeoPackage format) were loaded. An initial check ensured each polygon layer was projected to a consistent Coordinate ReferenceSystem (EPSG:6424) to align with the raster datasets.
2.  **Iterative Polygon Processing:** Each polygon was processed iteratively. For every individual polygon feature:
      - **Raster-Polygon Intersection:** The Rasterio’s “rasterio.mask.mask” function was utilized to extract only the raster pixel values that fell within the boundaries of the current polygon. This created a masked subset of the raster data specific to that polygon's geometry. An initial check for spatial disjointedness between the polygon and raster extents was performed to skip unnecessary processing.
      - **Statistical Summarization:**
          - For **continuous raster data** (e.g., temperature, elevation, dasymetric population), NumPy functions were applied to the extracted pixel values to calculate descriptive statistics. These included the count of valid pixels, mean, median, minimum, maximum, standard deviation, and variance (and sum for population) of the raster values within the polygon.
          - For **categorical raster data** (e.g., landcover classes, flood risk categories), the scripts would similarly extract pixel values. Then, pixel counts for each unique category within the polygon were determined. These counts were converted into percentages of the polygon's total area covered by each category, and absolute areas (e.g., square footage) after accounting for pixel resolution.
3.  **Attribute Appending:** The derived statistics (e.g., ECOSTRESS surface temperature mean, canopy percent, high flood risk square feet) were appended as new attributes to the corresponding polygon feature in the GeoDataFrame.
4.  **Output Generation:** Once all polygons were processed, the updated GeoDataFrame, now enriched with the aggregated raster statistics, was saved as a new GeoPackage file. 
This systematic approach ensured that each polygon feature was accurately attributed with summary statistics derived from the overlapping raster data, providing a quantitative basis for subsequent spatial analysis, map and chart generation, and reporting.
### 7.2.2 Polygon Aggregation Methods
In order to obtain metrics that combine attributes calculated at the parcel level with larger geometries (such as, for example, “square feet of residential pavement per watershed”), this study employs an areal interpolation methodology to aggregate polygon-specific attributes from a detailed parcel dataset to sets of larger, summarizing polygons such as h3 hexagons, CSAs, supervisorial districts and watersheds. This process allows for the estimation of values for a target set of polygons based on data from a source set of polygons where the boundaries do not perfectly align. The core of this analysis is performed using the GeoPandas library in Python, leveraging its spatial overlay and data manipulation capabilities. 
The primary steps of the methodology are as follows:
1.  **Data Ingestion and Preparation:** Two distinct polygon layers are read into GeoPandas GeoDataFrames: a detailed "parcels" layer containing various land use and development-related attributes, and a larger polygon layer representing the larger geographic units into which the parcel data will be aggregated. 
2.  **Areal Weighting through Spatial Overlay:** A spatial intersection overlay is performed between the parcels and the big polygons. This operation clips the parcels to the boundaries of the larger polygons, creating a new layer of intersection polygons. For each resulting intersection, the area is calculated. This intersection area is then used to determine a weight, which represents the proportion of each original parcel that falls within a given larger polygon.
3.  **Weighted Value Calculation:** The numeric attributes of interest from the original parcels are then weighted by this calculated proportion. This is achieved by multiplying the attribute value by the ratio of the intersection area to the original parcel's area. This step ensures that the contribution of each parcel's attributes to the larger polygon is proportional to its spatial extent within that polygon. This includes the aggregation of various pavement and landscape requirement metrics.
4.  **Categorical Data Aggregation:** For categorical attributes, such as land use descriptions, a different approach is taken. The weighted pavement area is calculated for each land use category within the intersection polygons. A pivot table is then created to transform the data, resulting in separate columns for the total weighted pavement area for each unique land use category within each large polygon.
5.  **Aggregation and Data Merging:** The weighted numeric attributes and the pivoted categorical data are then grouped by the unique identifier of the larger polygons and summed. This yields the total aggregated values for each larger polygon. These aggregated sums are subsequently joined back to the original larger polygons GeoDataFrame, effectively adding the newly calculated summary statistics as new columns.
## **7.3 Heuristic Methods**
### 7.3.1 Parking Requirements Method
The parking requirements for residential, commercial and industrial parcels were derived from Title 22 of the County code (referenced online in late 2024). These parking assumptions are shown in table 7.4. For residential we overstate the requirement by assuming generous 12’x24' spaces for residents with full-size pickup trucks (the actual County code requirement is for spaces to be around 8’6”x18’).
When we added up the total parking requirements for each of these three use categories using these assumptions, we found that the required parking amounted to 28% of non-road pavement for residential parcels, 23% for commercial, and 16% for industrial. We thus conservatively assumed that for all other parcel types (agricultural, institutional, recreational, government and miscellaneous), that the parking requirements were 30% of the non-road pavement, which, being based on our aerial imagery, does not account for subsurface, multilevel, or indoor parking. We used this conservative approach (rather than, for example, assuming 23%) so as not to exaggerate the amount of pavement that might be potentially non-core. This could be refined by more detailed correspondence of parcel land use codes with the Title 22 specifications, but this rough method did allow an overall order-of-magnitude estimation of the parking requirements for all parcels in the county, which could be subtracted from the parcel non-road pavement to yield a crude estimate of non-core pavement.

| :- | :- |
| **Residential Definition** | **Parking Area Required (sqft per parcel)** |
| Single Family | 576 |
| Two Units | 864 |
| Three Units | 864 |
| Four Units | 864 |
| Five or More Units | 1152 |
| Modular Home | 576 |
| Mobile Home | 576 |
| Rooming House | 576 |
| Mobile Home Park | 1152 |

Table 7.4: Assumptions for residential parking requirements
|  |  |
| :- | :- |
| **Industrial** | **Building sqft per Required 152 sqft Parking Space** |
| Warehouse | 1000 |
| Industrial | 500 |
| Offices (within industrial building) | 400 |
| **Commercial** |  |

##### **Table 7.5: Assumptions for parking requirements for commercial and industrial parcels.**
This also enabled us to estimate the total number of commercial and industrial parking spaces required in the County by summing the required area per parcel (which comes to 4,015,930). A 2015 Chester et al study estimated that in 2010 there were 12,303,349 total off street spaces, while our estimate is 7% lower at 11,433,288. The same group estimated in 2010 that, nationwide, the ratio of surface parking to structured parking (multilevel, subsurface etc) may range from 1:1 (in dense urban cores) to 7:1. Thus the 4,015,930 is still a conservative estimate of the total number of surface parking spaces available for heuristic estimates on the impact of parking lot depaving strategies discussed in Chapter 4. 
### 7.3.2 Sidewalks Requirements Method
To estimate the contribution of sidewalks to the interpretations of our dataset, we used the following method:
We employed the “tile2net” algorithm to estimate sidewalk polygons from high-resolution aerial imagery (provided by LARIAC) for three sample areas of the county. These sample areas included East LA, Boyle Heights, and Carson. This algorithm produces polygons around sidewalk areas in an aerial image, but because it is trained on networks of paths rather than strictly as image segmentation, it can extend sidewalk polygon estimations underneath tree canopy and building shadows. Figure 7.2 shows an example of the tile2net segmentation of an aerial image in the Carson neighborhood.
\[image\]
**Figure 7.2: An aerial image from Carson showing a purple outline where tile2net classifies sidewalk pixels.**
Figure 7.3 shows a more zoomed-in example of the tile2net sidewalk polygons compared to our pavement classification.
\[image\]
**Figure 7.3: a) shows the tile2net sidewalk polygons as purple outlines over the aerial image. b) are the same sidewalk outlines in purple over the land classification.**
As can be seen in Figure 7.3a, the tile2net algorithm, while not perfect, is largely effective at identifying where sidewalks are located and then separating them from roads and crosswalks, even when they run underneath tree canopy. As can be seen in Figure 7.3b, the sidewalks often appear to cover road pixels rather than non-road pavement pixels. 
To estimate sidewalk locations across the County, we started by running tile2net on a 6,651 acre sample area in the vicinity of Boyle Heights. Tile2net also provides line geometry for all sidewalks in the sample area, and we calculated the total length of these to be 321 miles. We then used the OSM overpass API to collect all of the road lines (using network\_type=’drive’ to exclude service roads, parking aisles, and alleys) from the same sample area. We calculated these to be 461 miles long. Thus the average ratio of sidewalks to road lengths is around 0.7. We then calculated the entire length of all roads in the County (42,835 miles), and multiplied this by 0.7 to estimate the total length of sidewalks in the County (29,984 miles). We then multiplied this by 4 feet to get a total square footage of sidewalks that might be considered core (14,538 acres). To relate this to the pavement classification pixels, we focused on the inter-parcel space, or right- of- way (ROW), which is where most sidewalks appear when overlaying the ROW boundary with aerial imagery, and where 70% of the tile2net sidewalk polygon area in our sample datasets fell. 
Using a sample of 1,884 acres of sidewalk from a tile2net test in a section of Boyle Heights, we found that overall around 15% of the pixels in the sidewalk polygons were road pixels, while 53% were NRP pixels. We also found that of the sidewalk area, 70% was in the right-of-way rather than inside parcels. Of the sidewalks in the right-of-way, we found that 20% of the pixels were road pixels, and 52% were NRP pixels. 
These figures derived from this rubric were used to dial in estimates of how much ROW NRP is sidewalks, and how much pavement classified as road in the ROW is actually potentially sidewalk. Thus the non-core pavement data is considered a high level estimate for gauging “order-of-magnitude” opportunities and priorities for depaving. Additional geospatial analysis and machine learning can be done to further specify in greater details the distribution of sidewalks in future study phases. 
These are very rough estimates and could be refined further by more detailed surveys of the sidewalk to pavement pixel class apportionment, and by deep learning analysis targeting sidewalks specifically. 
### 7.3.3 Landscape Potential Heuristics
#### **Commercial Landscape Potential**
The Los Angeles County Planning and Zoning Code 22.20.040 stipulates that commercial parcels permitted after 2019 must be at least 10% landscaped. While this requirement only applies to newer commercial parcels in unincorporated areas, it represents a heuristic target that we can apply to all commercial parcels for the purposes of defining aspirational depaving targets. 
#### **Residential Landscape Potential**
The August 2024 amendment to Title 22 – Planning and Zoning of the Los Angeles
County Code – includes design standards for residential development. One of the included requirements is that 20% of the area not in use for buildings in residential parcels be landscaped, up to 5,000 square feet. While this only technically would apply to new developments in unincorporated areas, we can use this figure as a benchmark for residential landscape area requirements. Each parcel’s landscape area was calculated from the landcover statistics by summing the tree canopy, low vegetation, medium vegetation, bare earth, and water square footage. We then divided this by the total parcel area (after first subtracting building area from it) to get the existing landscaped area percentage for each parcel. These per parcel landscape area calculations were used to develop the depaving targets discussed in section 7.3.4. 
### 7.3.4 Public Parcel Ownership Category
We analysed 49,807 publicly owned parcels using the “Owner Full Name” field (e.g., “AGOURA HILLS CITY CLERK”). By scanning that text for diagnostic keywords we assigned every parcel to one and only one of eight ownership classes, as shown in table 7.6.
|  |  |
| :- | :- |
| **Category** | **Keyword Rule** |
| County | "LA COUNTY\|LA CO \|CO SAN\|LACMTA\|LOS ANGELES COUNTY\|COUNTY SAN\|COUNTY OF LOS\|L A COUNTY\|L A CO " |
| School | "SCHOOL\|COLLEGE\|UNIVERSITY" |
| Parks | "PARK\|REC\|CONSERV\|GARDEN" |
| Water | "WATER\|IRRIG\|HYDRO\|SANIT" |
| City | "CITY" |
| State | “STATE OF CA” |
| Federal | "U S GOVT\|U S GOV'T\|US GOV\|UNITED STATE\|POSTAL" |
| Other | (records not caught by the rules above). |

Table 7.6: Ownership categories and keyword rules used for public parcel ownership analysis.
To avoid double-counting, the rules are applied hierarchically: for instance, a parcel named “LOS ANGELES CITY WATER DISTRICT” is counted in Water / Sanitation, not in City, and a state-owned park appears in Parks & Recreation, not in State. Figure 3.10 shows how pavement area is distributed across these eight public-ownership categories.
### 7.3.5 Intersections
We used the python OSMNX library to download all of the drivable roads as edges and their intersections as nodes for graph analysis. Some edges have user-provided lane counts. We summed the number of lane counts per intersection and divided it by the number of streets connected to the node to get the average number of lanes per connected street. We removed nodes with average lanes per street less than 3 to get “wide” intersections. Of 174,633 intersections, only 74,751 (43%) had lane information, and of those, 332 were considered “wide.” For each street connected to each node we assumed 2 corners, each of which could have the 250 square feet of pavement removed according to the adaptation strategy. We then divided by 43% to extrapolate across the nodes missing lane information, to get 54 acres of pavement that could be removed from wide intersections.

## **7.4 Limitations of These Methods**
Conducting a wall-to-wall depaving assessment for all 4,000 + square miles of Los Angeles County is necessarily an exercise in balancing breadth and depth. The limitations in table 7.7 should be kept in mind whenever countywide results are interpreted or applied to site-scale decisions.
|  |  |  |
| :- | :- | :- |
| **Category** | **Key Sources of Uncertainty** | **Practical Implication** |
| **Spatial Resolution** | • 0.75 ft (≈23 cm) pixels are coarse relative to many real-world features (planter strips, narrow medians, curbs, lane markings) | Fine-grained elements of the public realm, such as “tenths” of a parking stall, micro-swales, or thin sidewalk buffers, are often omitted or mis-classified, leading to conservative (under-counted) pavement totals. |
| **Mutually Exclusive Land Cover Classes** | • Single-class assignment per pixel masks mixed surfaces (e.g., asphalt under tree canopy) | Conservative (under-counted) pavement |
| **Temporal Mismatch** | • Land classification sourced from 2023-2024 imagery, ECOSTRESS surface temperature from 2024, flood confidence model from 2006 DEM, parcels from 2025 | Any location that changed appreciably between vintages will carry mixed or outdated attributes, introducing temporal bias into aggregate statistics. |
| **Classification Error** | Landcover classification has assessed overall accuracy of 83%, which means all pavement amounts may be + or - 17% | This baseline might not allow change detection over time for changes \<17% with much confidence. |
| **Polygon Aggregation** | • Parcels and ROW derived from assessor geometry; internal lot lines inside campuses and shopping centers introduce slivers of ROW that are not functionally roadway    • Parcel data aggregated to other geometries was "weighted"    • School buffering heuristic merges adjacent parcels but ignores inter-parcel ROW | Pavement attributed to “ROW” may include drive aisles or parking lots that function as private space; conversely, contiguous school grounds may be split, understating on-site imperviousness. Parcel level data aggregated into other small geometries is approximate. |
| **Parcel Use Code Ambiguity** | Parcel use codes may not be updated or accurate | For example, "government use - unspecified" use code includes parcels across diverse uses that may have varying opportunity |
| **Heuristic Methods** | • Title 22 parking rules applied at broad UseCode groupings; multilevel or subsurface parking not captured    • Parking heuristic based on County code and not on the 88 cities    • Sidewalk length extrapolated from one Boyle Heights sample using tile2net and OSM road ratio | Parking requirements likely over-state necessity on small residential parcels and under-state supply at large commercial centers. Sidewalk square footage requirement likely overestimated overall, and may vary significantly in hillside or industrial districts. |
| **Model Generalization** | • Flood confidence, temperature exposure, and population density raster layers each carry their own modeling assumptions    • H3 hexagon, watershed, and CSA boundaries impose arbitrary cut-offs that may bisect real-world systems | Risk-oriented prioritization (e.g., heat + population + pavement) inherits compound uncertainty; cross-boundary projects (e.g., river corridors) may require custom aggregation. |

Table 7.7: Selected limitations of the methods used for this assessment.
#### **Many of these limitations point towards the necessity of site-scale follow-up when moving beyond the county-scale. For example:**
1.  **Granular design constraints**: Driveway throat widths, utility clearances, ADA turning radii, and emergency vehicle easements are all sub-pixel features invisible to this study.  
      
2.  **Contextual land-use nuance**: A 20,000 ft² “industrial” parcel in Vernon may host a parking-intensive logistics yard, whereas the same UseCode in Pasadena could be a  multi-story light-manufacturing building with negligible surface parking; the county-wide heuristics average these extremes.  
      
3.  **Partner and equity considerations**: Ownership patterns, community priorities, and planned capital projects determine whether an area’s theoretical depaving potential is socially and politically feasible. These factors require qualitative engagement beyond the raster analytics used for this assessment.
These countywide datasets can be a powerful screening tool; it can highlight hotspots, rank jurisdictions, and gauge order-of-magnitude benefits. **Yet any move from *****potential***** to *****action***** should trigger a sequence of more specific assessments: higher-resolution data, field checks, and partner dialogue tailored to the unique spatial, regulatory, and social context of each prospective depaving site.**
## **7.5 Opportunities for Refinement**
The methods used in this assessment could be improved upon for future assessments. The following methodological improvements can be leveraged for future depaving assessments to track progress of ongoing depaving efforts:
1.  **Landcover rasters should be generated with a purpose-built model (fine-tuned for pavement specific assessment using 4 inch imagery). **This would improve the accuracy of the data.
1.  **Tile2net should be run for the whole County to get a specific measurement of actual sidewalks.** This would provide a much more accurate baseline for sidewalk square footage than the current estimation method, which relies on a single sample area.
1.  **The parking heuristic should be exhaustively derived from each of the city codes in addition to the County code, with the resulting requirements applied to City-specific parcels**. This would create a more accurate and spatially refined estimation of required parking across the entire county. Future research could also incorporate more dynamic parking demand models that consider factors like time of day, day of the week, and local events to move beyond static, requirements-based estimations.
1.  **Advanced remote sensing methods for measuring parking spaces should be used to ascertain how many stalls are 90 degrees vs other angles. **This would enable quantification of potential benefits of scaling parking lot depaving strategies.
1.  **Integrate qualitative and community-based data.** To better understand the social and political feasibility of depaving projects, future assessments should incorporate qualitative data. This could include data from community meetings, surveys, and interviews to capture local knowledge, priorities, and concerns. Combining this qualitative data with the quantitative geospatial analysis will provide a more holistic understanding of depaving opportunities and challenges.
1.  **Develop a more nuanced understanding of public parcel use.** While the current study categorizes public parcels by owner, future analyses could further disaggregate these categories to better understand the specific uses of public land. For instance, "City" owned parcels could be further classified into functional categories like "municipal buildings," "public works yards," and "community centers" to better identify specific depaving opportunities.
1.  **Include infiltration and surface flow data in prioritization. **The SSURGO data and recent developments in surface flow modeling can be used to prioritize depaving based on where infiltration will be most beneficial for both flood mitigation and groundwater recharge. 
1.  **Model UTCI at high resolution for heat assessments.** New methods for physical modeling enable countywide estimation of thermal comfort metrics taking into account the shade of trees and buildings, and the impact of wind. These simulations would afford a more specific estimate of heat stress than surface temperature. 
1.  **Needs assessment should include vulnerability metrics. **The needs assessment should be expanded to include vulnerability metrics such as the CDC/ATSDR Social Vulnerability Index, the EPA Community Health Vulnerability Index (CHVI), the EDF Climate Vulnerability Index (CVI) and similar indicators to include the consideration of receptor sensitivity with the physical measurements and models for the evaluation of pavement removal needs. Because such metrics are often limited in spatial resolution to the census block geometry, dasymetric methods can be used to harmonize them with the hex grids used in this study. 


# Scraps

This chapter will provide guidance for design and implementation of depaving projects. First we review existing design standards that relate to pavement, and how they relate to depaving. We then offer a design toolkit of “depaving strategies” for tailoring depaving to the various site conditions found throughout the County. Next, we present a high-level cost analysis and recommendations for engaging contractors and communities to ensure equitable and sustainable outcomes. We then combine the depaving strategies with the pavement analysis data to extrapolate the potential quantities of pavement that could be removed using selected strategies. We conclude by offering depaving targets for municipal agencies as well as a framework for setting them.

### 7.3.4 Depaving Targets
For the preliminary targets shown in Appendix B, we used the following method: Residential and commercial landscape targets are based on 100% of the landscape requirements discussed in chapters 5.4.1 and 7.3.3. The Industrial target is 1% of the industrial pavement in the 50th percentile tier 1 hotspots. The vacant target is all of the vacant pavement in the all hotpots tiers. The ROW target is 10% of non-core ROW pavement in all hotspot tiers. The school target is based on the following:
##### **Schools target**
The Los Angeles Unified School District (LAUSD) Green Schoolyards for All Plan identifies 475 schools in their district that require upgrades to meet their greenness goals, with a collective pavement replacement requirement of 12.3 million square feet (282 acres). Of all the 3179 schools in our countywide schools dataset, 1379 are in the LAUSD. The remaining 1,800 are in other districts. The 425 schools that LAUSD identified as having excess pavement represent around 30% of the total LAUSD schools. If we extrapolate the same percentage of non-LAUSD schools, we get 540 additional schools. Using the same required depaving per school as the LAUSD (an average of 28,941 square feet per school) we can aspire to remove 15,628,235 square feet (358 acres) from non LAUSD schools. Combined with the LAUSD target we get a total schools depaving target of 27,928,235 square feet (641 acres) of school pavement.

### **Chapter 6: Summary and Recommendations**
In Chapter 6, the report concludes with a summary of our key findings, and proposes an initial series of policy recommendations. Beyond aligning depaving efforts with existing County sustainability initiatives such as the CFMP, Vision Zero, and the Safe Clean Water Program (SCWP), we also propose novel mechanisms for cross-agency collaboration on depaving. Notably, we recommend the County integrating a “Depaving First” principle in forthcoming design standards, RFPs, and grant scoring criteria. Rather than treating pavement removal as an optional add-on, it should be a required practice in meeting the County’s climate adaptation goals. We further hope that County departments (assisted by communities and NGOs) will leverage our tools to develop implementation plans, incentive programs, and update existing planning code evaluations and design standards.
### **Chapter 7: Methodology**
The report’s findings are underpinned by a rigorous geospatial land use classification process. Pavement was disaggregated from other impervious land cover types such as buildings, and classified into specific categories like roads, sidewalks, and parking areas. The pavement types were then re-aggregated into geographies such as parcels, communities, watersheds, and supervisorial districts. This section details the methodologies used in providing data to chapters 2, 3, 4, and 5, while also addressing limitations and opportunities for future refinement.

**RF Comment: all this text seems out of place; i'd consider deleting this. it is more interesting context for us as the creators of this assessment than info that is needed by the reader.**
A report published in 2020 by Columbia University* *entitled* “Heat Vulnerability in Los Angeles County: Resource and Methodology Assessment” *evaluates whether existing datasets, data-sharing platforms, and interagency tools are sufficient to track, prioritize, and implement these goals. Rather than conducting original data analysis, the report inventories and assesses a suite of tools including the Climate-Smart Cities platform (Trust for Public Land), NASA’s ECOSTRESS imagery, the Fourth California Climate Assessment, Cal-Adapt, the California Heat Assessment Tool (CHAT), Climate Resolve’s *Ready for Tomorrow*, CalBRACE, and the California Healthy Places Index (HPI).
The Assessment concluded that many existing datasets are limited by coarse spatial resolution, making them insufficient for site-specific planning. Moreover, most tools surveyed lack parcel-level actionable intelligence, preventing planners from pinpointing precise locations where interventions like pavement removal or tree planting would have the greatest impact. Tools often lack the ability to overlay multiple vulnerability indicators or risks simultaneously. This limits cross-sectoral planning. While tools like Climate-Smart Cities and the California Heat Assessment Tool (CHAT) begin to integrate exposure and sensitivity to support spatial prioritization, the report notes a persistent gap in understanding how multiple vulnerability factors combine to identify the highest-need areas. Existing tools also lack seamless integration across land use, infrastructure, and public health factors, requiring further methodological development to guide coordinated investment. There is thus an urgent need for a tool that combines high-resolution, parcel-level geospatial analysis of impervious service and land use typologies with feasibility indicators and implementation strategies.

**From Chapter 2: **
LA County’s 2024 CFMP set out to “prioritize park and street tree resources for high canopy need communities.” To calculate “high canopy need,” the County first narrowed its spatial analysis to parks, ROW, commercial, and residential land uses, while excluding open space, agriculture, and manufacturing, including aircraft and heavy manufacturing, to calculate what it called “adjusted canopy cover.” It then combined these metrics with factors such as age, occupation, health, income, and access to information, all grouped under a “social sensitivity index” (SSI). The resulting list of communities prioritized for tree planting were then ranked in order of low, medium, and high “canopy need” across the County’s unincorporated communities. These communities included Athens Westmont, Lake Los Angeles, and East Los Angeles among others (see sub-chapter 2.7, Figure X, for a complete list of communities ranked in the high need category). 
The CFMP Tree Canopy Equity Target aims to achieve at least 20% canopy cover across all unincorporated areas, with a separate goal of 15% canopy cover in each individual unincorporated community, a notable increase from the current baseline where only 37% of residents live in areas meeting this threshold. The Street Tree Target sets a countywide goal of 95% stocking rate, up from the current 85%, while the Park Tree Target seeks to halt the net loss of park trees, reversing the current projectionof losing approximately 650 trees per year. Lastly, the Local Tree Supply Target aspires to source 100% of County trees from local nurseries, with the baseline still being established as part of an initial implementation step. All targets are based on a 50-year projection and reflect a long-term commitment to environmental justice, climate resilience, and local economic development.
The October 8 CFMP motion enacts this tree planting mandate by directing departments to implement extended watering for new street trees, pilot depaving projects in high-need communities, launch an equity-based residential tree planting program, and identify tree canopy expansion opportunities in parks. 
The targets of the 2019 OurCounty Sustainability Plan intersect with those of the CFMP in calling for the conversion of 10% of heat-trapping surfaces to cool or green surfaces by 2025, 20% by 2035, and 30% by 2045. It also sets a countywide goal to increase urban tree canopy cover by 10% of its 2016 baseline by 2025, 15% by 2035, and 20% by 2045. As previously mentioned, the updated OurCounty Plan, currently out for public comment, includes some of the depaving targets from this report.
**From ES Sept 1st:**
### Integration into Countywide Plans & Programs
*Depaving needs cross department support*
#### **Plans & Programs**
**Finding: **
  - Despite widespread alignment with County goals, depaving is not yet leveraged to meet the numerous different planning initiatives that are possible.
  - Depaving is underleveraged in County sustainability and infrastructure plans & programs like Vision Zero and SCWP.
**Recommendation: **
  - Include depaving solutions in RFPs and planning frameworks. Require a **“Depaving Evaluation”** in all County RFPs and capital project templates
  - Leverage this depaving data and insights in the OurCounty Plan, Climate Action Plan, Heat Action Plan, and future plans as they are developed.
  - Develop future **depaving targets** based on benchmarks and needs assessment goals.
  - Adopt a “Depaving First” standard across County projects. 
  - Require a “Depaving Evaluation” in all RFPs and update design standards to pair depaving with nature-based solutions (e.g., tree wells, bioswales, green corridors).
  - Adapt SCWP program to prioritize land transformation through depaving.
#### **Design Standards**
**Finding: **
  - Current standards for parking dimensions, drive aisle widths, and ROW layout limit space for greening. Current design standards for Vision Zero sometimes remove pavement, but could be further explored.
**Recommendation**: 
  - Update codes to support compact/angled parking, reduce curb radii, and enable modular tree well systems, freeing space for trees, infiltration, and cooling infrastructure.
  - **Update design manuals** to prioritize tree wells, bioswales, and stormwater BMPs wherever hardscape is removed.
  - **Encourage integrated block-scale strategies**, such as reducing travel lanes to create planted medians, reconfiguring parking lots with tree islands and bioswales, and adding hedgerows or vegetated buffers along residential frontages, to multiply benefits across parcels and right-of-way.
  - Develop Toolkits provide assistance with site assessment, design guidelines, permitting pathways, O\&M strategies, and guidance for securing future funding. 
#### **Hotspots & Environmental Justice**
**Finding**: 
  - More than 1,500 acres of pavement are in areas simultaneously experiencing extreme heat, flood risk, pavement burden, and low tree canopy. 
  - According to the SB535 definition of Disadvantaged Communities, they contain 147,487 acres of total pavement (approximately half of the total), of which 65,905 acres are thought to not be in use as roads or sidewalks, or required for parking.
**Recommendation **
  - High-Need “Hotspot” Areas Should Be Prioritized for Depaving, and this emphasis should be worked into other planning initiatives. 
### Interdepartmental Coordination & Governance
*Depaving Should Be Tracked, Funded & Coordinated*
**Finding: **
  - SCWP or general operating funds from key agencies are not directed to Depaving, but could be to meet multiple program goals.
  - Depaving often requires different departments with different agendas and goals, some of which don't directly benefit from depaving.
**Recommendation: **
  - Empower the recently formed Depaving (CED) subgroup to coordinate depaving integration across agencies and planning initiatives.
  - Appoint a lead to advance implementation, track progress, and align funding across agencies. 
  - County Supervisors and Department Managers should encourage staff to identify and remove roadblocks.
### Pilot Projects
*Public Projects could help align the process. Private project could offer offer Quick Wins*
**Finding**: 
  - Public Parcels contain 38,225 acres of pavement, and publicly owned Rights of Way between parcels contain 125,565 acres of pavement. 
  - Vacant parcels alone contain over 11,000 acres of pavement, while government and school-owned parcels offer high-leverage opportunities under public control.
**Recommendation:**
  - The County should develop and launch a *Depaving Pilot Program*, which was also a specific recommendation in the 2024 motion to approve the CFMP. 
  - Consider funding Residential and Commercial Depaving Pilot Projects
  - Pilot depaving projects on public lands, particularly vacant parcels and in Rights of Way as an early intervention opportunity, in alignment with the motion to do depaving pilot projects, in the CFMP plan approved by the Board of Supervisors in Fall 2024. 
  - Direct early investments and pilot programs toward stacked-needs hotspots and otherwise disadvantaged areas, including Vision Zero corridors and schools in high-vulnerability zones.
### Incentives Program
*Residential, Commercial, and Industrial Sites need Incentives*
**Finding**: 
  - Of all the pavement in the County that is not in use as roads or thought to be required for parking or sidewalks, 70% (88,374 acres) of it lies within privately owned parcels. Yet private owners lack sufficient incentives to depave, especially where excess parking or hardscape is the norm.
  - Private parcels will be needed to address the multiple needs like heat,  stormwater management.
  - This assessment identifies depaving targets based on need and land use type
**Recommendation: **
  - The County should develop a *Depaving Incentive Program* for private properties to encourage retrofits of private properties. 
  - The incentive program could be funded with a combination of funds from existing funding streams such as: SCWP, Vision Zero, Measure A , as well as grant opportunities such as the Prop 4 Climate Bond. 
  - Initial targets have been provided to OurCounty. These targets should be improved with refined analysis in the future. Depaving pilot programs should leverage land use and needs data in this report to execute a diverse set of pilot projects to gather data for full development of depaving initiatives. These initiatives can be funded by new incentive programs geared towards hitting depaving targets. 
  - Some incentives should focus on multifamily properties, parking lots, and high-intensity industrial sites
  - Incentivize public vacant parcel depaving particularly in high needs areas, ideally to serve as pocket park. Incentivize purchase of  private parcels or purchase of parcels by public agencies. 
### Schools 
*Schools Are Critical to Climate Resilience and the most sensitive receptors*
**Finding: **
  - School Campuses contain \> 14000 acres of pavement
**Recommendation: **
  - Support implementation of LAUSD’s 30% greening target through interagency coordination, technical toolkits, and soil safety classification frameworks.
  - Partner with LAUSD, LACOE, and other L.A. school districts to allocate greening funds toward depaving and nature-based play amenities to create greener, cooler campuses.
  - Green Schools America has done a study of what pavement is in school play areas vs parking lots, which is forthcoming. That data would help better target depaving. Additionally, the County or Schools need to conduct a study and develop guidelines for active play area needs for which surfaces need pavement and which do not.
  - Support school districts and provide technical assistance to school districts for aligning schoolyard design with both educational and climate goals by integrating depaving, tree canopy, and permeable play surfaces.
### Adaptive Management
*Depaving Los Angeles County is a massive, complex project which must be managed as such. *
**Findings: **
  - Depaving at the County scale requires solving complicated problems across agencies, disciplines, and partnerships. 
  - This depaving assessment does not go into detail about what specific pavement can be removed, as more data gathering is required
  - To maintain accountability in meeting depaving targets, depaving data will need to be updated to keep track of progress, limitations, and lessons learned. 
**Recommendations:**
  - Depaving actors should pool resources across partnerships to maintain a coordinated approach so that, as higher quality data is generated, it can be made available to others.
  - Depaving projects should go through an iterative cycle of design, modeling, implementation, and evaluation. The resulting ROIs and iterative design improvements should be then fed into the next cycle of design, on a larger set of projects.
  - Depaving assessments should be repeated every 3 years to update data and reconsolidate project learnings. 
**From Chatper 6 August 27:**
In our spatial analysis of over two million county parcels, we identified key distribution, intensity, and ownership patterns to guide pavement removal strategies. The largest total acreage of pavement is concentrated in three land use categories: Residential, Industrial, and Government. Building on these insights, this chapter lays out an initial policy framework to begin institutionalizing pavement removal as a green infrastructure tool. 
The chapter opens with the highlights of our key findings, followed by an overview of some proposed policy recommendations. It then offers a deeper synthesis of key findings from each chapter, culminating in a phased framework to help L.A. County initiate a coordinated, cross-departmental depaving strategy.
**Finding:**
School districts and universities control approximately 24% of all pavement on public parcels in Los Angeles County, making them key targets for strategic depaving. 
**Recommendation:**
Prioritize pavement removal on school and university properties to reduce heat exposure and enhance student health. Leverage these campuses as climate resilience hubs by promoting joint-use agreements that provide shaded, greened spaces for surrounding communities during extreme heat events.
**Finding**:
Vacant industrial and commercial parcels often exhibit exceptionally high pavement intensity. Despite contributing fewer total acres than some other land use categories, these parcels represent high-opportunity sites for depaving because they can be transformed without disrupting active uses. 
**Recommendation**:
Develop a coordinated policy to prioritize depaving on vacant or low-activity industrial and commercial parcels, especially those with high pavement intensity and overlap with heat, flood, and canopy vulnerabilities. Local jurisdictions should integrate these parcels into stormwater planning and resilience investments, particularly when sites are publicly owned or lightly used.
**From executive summary August 27: **
### Proposed Targets:
**I. Commercial & Residential Target (10,485 Acres of pavement removal)**
  - **Analyze:** We assessed every parcel's current land cover and required parking area to determine its "landscape percentage."
  - **Set Goals:** We set targets for parcels that fell below a desired landscape percentage, while still retaining pavement needed to meet parking requirements:
      - **Residential:** 40% Landscaped Area
      - **Commercial:** 10% Landscaped Area
  - **Prioritize:** The analysis was focused *exclusively* on parcels within high-need areas (top 20% for heat or flood risk, or bottom 20% for tree canopy).
**II. Schools Target (641 Acres of pavement removal)**
  - This target was calculated by using the LAUSD Green Schoolyards plan as a benchmark and extrapolating the average pavement removal per school to all schools county-wide.
Depaving Target:** 11,126 Acres**
  - **Residential: 9,297 acres**
  - **Commercial:** **1,188 acres**
  - **Schools:** **641 acres**
Note that the draft OurCounty Sustainability Plan currently being circulated for public comment includes a depaving target of **1600 acres for public property and schools** (as a direct result of this work). 
**From Chapter 6.1 August 26th 2025 around 4pm:**
#### **Tier 1: Tactical (Most Readily Implementable)**
  - **Street Closure and Parklet Installations:** Temporarily or permanently close small street segments to vehicular traffic, install planters, and create public gathering spaces to generate neighborhood buy-in for depaving.  
  - **Sidewalk Bioswale Retrofits:** Integrate small-scale bioswales and tree wells into existing sidewalks and pedestrian zones.
  - **Planted Bulbouts at Wide Intersections**: Convert underused corner paving into planted bulbouts with low vegetation and curb cuts for stormwater capture. These simple, highly visible interventions offer immediate pedestrian safety improvements, support Vision Zero goals, and provide an accessible entry point for cities new to depaving.
  - **Mini-mall Parking Lot Enhancements:** Pilot small-scale interventions in strip mall lots, such as bioswales, hedgerows, and tree planting without large-scale regrading.
  - **Vacant Parcel Depaving Demonstrations:** Target vacant commercial and industrial parcels with high pavement intensity for early pilot depaving and greening projects. These sites are often underutilized and offer an opportunity for rapid implementation without displacing active uses. Pilot projects can showcase climate and stormwater benefits while informing design standards and incentive structures for broader adoption.
-----
#### **Tier 2: Intermediate (Moderate Ambition)**
  - **Angled Parking Conversions:** Convert existing parallel parking to angled layouts in commercial and industrial districts. This could enable removal of up to 2,519 football field–sized portions of pavement and create space for at least one small tree per four required parking spaces across approximately 4 million spaces.  
  - **Median and Parkway Retrofits:** Replace paved medians and wide parkways with vegetated buffers and tree plantings to enhance infiltration and reduce urban heat.  
  - **Schoolyard and Playground Depaving:** Remove excess pavement in schoolyards and convert surfaces to green play areas, canopy cover, and community gardens.
  - **Compact Parking Conversions:** Convert a portion of standard parking spaces to compact spaces. Converting just 25% of the estimated 4 million commercial and industrial parking spaces could free up pavement equivalent to 575 football fields, while responding to shifting vehicle trends (e.g., 34% of new car sales were compact or subcompact in 2024).
  - **Landscape Ratio Compliance in Commercial Parcels:** Implement pilot projects on commercial parcels to meet or exceed the County’s 10% landscape area requirement. This could collectively remove thousands of acres of excess hardscape and expand green space in business districts.  
  - **Tree Well Installations in ROW:** Target underutilized right-of-way areas (such as medians, bulbouts, and wide shoulders) for large-scale tree well retrofits. Converting just 10% of these areas could create over 1.3 million new tree wells across the County.
-----
#### **Tier 3: Transformative (Most Ambitious)**
  - **Integrated Greenway Redevelopments:** Transform entire street segments into shared or non-vehicular green corridors, including bioswales, tree wells, and permeable surfaces.  
  - **Major Parking Lot Reconfigurations:** Redevelop large commercial or industrial parking lots to reduce paved area through compact and angled parking layouts, combined with vegetated medians and infiltration zones.
**Some Chapter 6 Scraps from Fletch on August 26th 2025 at around 2pm:**
### 6.2.1 Needs Assessment Findings
**The findings from Needs Assessment can be found in detail in chapter 2.8**
  - The analysis maps pavement burden, heat exposure, flood risk, and canopy gaps across all of L.A. County at a granular (70-meter hexagon) scale.
  - Risk layers are population-weighted and evaluated by quartile, highlighting the top 25% most affected areas for each factor.  
  - Areas where multiple risks overlap—so-called “stacked needs” hotspots—are prioritized for high-impact multi-benefit depaving interventions  
  - School sites and Vision Zero corridors are flagged where they intersect with these hotspots as prime opportunities to align depaving with existing funding streams.  
  - Supervisorial District maps identify within-district hotspots to prioritize capital planning.   
  - Hotspots are categorized into tiers based on pavement intensity and the number of overlapping environmental burdens.  
  - Key correlations found: heat with low canopy, and flood risk with pavement; overlaps between heat and flood were less common.
\[6.2.2 Needs Assessment Recommendations\]
  - 2.2.1 (pavement burden)
  - 2.2.4 (highest pavement burden at schools)
  - 2.2.5 (pavement in Vision Zero road segments)
  - 2.3.1 (most heat-burdened communities)
  - 2.3.3 (most heat-burdened schools)
  - 2.3.4 (most heat-burdened Vision Zero road segments)
  - 2.4.1 (communities most at risk of flooding)
  - 2.4.3 (schools most at risk of flooding)
  - 2.4.4 (Vision Zero road segments most at risk of flooding)
  - 2.5.1 (communities ranked lowest in tree canopy cover)
  - 2.5.5 (lowest canopy cover in Vision Zero road segments). 
  - For the Stacked Needs Communities and their specific hotspots, as well as stacking tiers, see 2.6.
### 6.3.1 Pavement Assessment Findings
Chapter 3 presents L.A. County’s first multidimensional map of pavement, categorized by **total area**, **intensity**, **geography**, and **land control**.
**The County** contains nearly **300,000 acres** of pavement, split roughly *equally* among:
  - Private parcels
  - Public parcels
  - Public right-of-way (ROW)  
**Residential parcels** have the **largest total paved area**.  
**Commercial** and **industrial** **parcels** are **more** **pavement-*****intensive***, however, especially:
  - Parking lots
  - Storage yards
  - Vacant parcels  
A **dual metric—total paved area vs. paved share per parcel**—helps locate both:
  - Where the most pavement exists
  - Where people and ecosystems feel the greatest burden  
**High-opportunity sites for depaving** include:
  - **Vacant** industrial/commercial parcels
  - **“Unspecified”** government-owned land
  - **Wide ROW medians or shoulders** (often publicly controlled)  
We also map pavement burden by:
  - **Supervisorial districts** (e.g., SD1, SD2, SD4 = industrial pavement concentration)
  - **Watersheds** (e.g., Lower L.A. River and Santa Monica Bay = flood-prone pavements)
  - **School districts** (schools and universities = \~25% of public parcel pavement, ideal for joint-use greening)
### 6.4.1 Pavement Needs Findings
Building on Chapter 3’s maps, this chapter introduces a **rule-of-thumb method** to estimate **how much pavement might not be removable**.  
Pavement is categorized into:
  - **Core pavement** (e.g., roads, ADA sidewalks, required parking)
  - Non-core pavement (not included in core pavement)  
Key findings:
  - **42%** of pavement **countywide** is classified as **non-core**
  - Most non-core pavement is on **private parcels**, not public streets  
This framework helps:
  - **Prioritize** sites for feasibility studies and pilot projects
  - **Screen** for where investment and design efforts could begin  
Areas with the highest non-core pavement include:
  - **Unincorporated** regions
  - **Hotspots** with overlapping climate and equity needs  
While based on rough assumptions, this approach is useful for guiding early action and planning.
Notably, we found strong correlations between heat and low canopy, and between flood risk and pavement, though cross-category overlaps (e.g., heat + flood) were less common. To guide intervention, we have categorized these hotspots into a tiered system based on pavement intensity and combination of multiple needs.
By combining population-weighted quartiles of pavement burden, heat exposure, flood risk, and canopy gaps at a 70-meter hexagon scale, the analysis then proceeds to identify discrete hotspots where environmental burdens stack. These “stacked needs” areas, where multiple top-quartile risk factors converge are highly specific zones that warrant targeted depaving investigations. 
, and L.A. County Water Plan
We then take the depaving strategies and explore how scaling them or extrapolating them across the County might play out in terms of how much pavement would be removed by layering on interpretive models that estimate the practicality and policy relevance of different depaving strategies. 
Our *Pavement Needs Analysis* section builds on the spatial pavement analysis presented in Chapter 3 by applying planning and zoning level land use requirements to the data from pavement analysis to generate heuristic estimation of parking area requirements, sidewalk area requirements, and landscape area requirements to explore the possible upper bounds of depaving opportunity; how much of the pavement found in chapter 3 needs to be retained for roads, sidewalks and parking, and how much might be less essential, and hence potentially considered for removability.
As shown in the image on the left, mid-century observers often glorified this car-oriented vision. But not so long ago, Los Angeles looked very different. The image on the right, for example, shows a tree-lined Santa Monica Boulevard around 1900. That earlier landscape reminds us that today’s pavement conceals the possibility of a more intentional and regenerative urban ecology.

The *DepaveLA* report presents the **first countywide, parcel- and right-of-way–level** pavement analysis for Los Angeles. Building on aerial imagery and land cover data, the analysis distinguishes between roads, other pavement, and estimates parking and sidewalks.
Using **high resolution pavement classification methods** and planning heuristics, the study maps both total pavement coverage and sorts the pavement into core pavement and non-core pavement: an estimate of what could be depaved under ideal conditions. Critically, the analysis integrates metrics of **heat exposure, flood risk, tree canopy gaps, and pavement area**, paving the way for composite prioritization indices for environmental justice–aligned intervention.
The scope encompasses **all of L.A. County**, not just unincorporated areas, and is designed to support decision-making across public agencies, municipalities, school districts, nonprofits, and private landowners.

## **Implementation Roadmap**
The report proposes the following 10-step roadmap to guide a first phase of policy adoption and project execution. 
1.  **Launch a fully funded 5-Year Countywide Depaving Pilot Program in FY2025** using Measure W allocations, prioritizing shovel-ready sites and interdepartmental pilots. Include dedicated technical assistance and evaluation frameworks.  
2.  **Publish and promote the Depaving Adaptation Toolkit**, including visual specifications, flowcharts to guide project selection by land use, and compliance checklists. Offer department-level trainings and integrate toolkit content into relevant capital project RFPs to support implementation at the staff level.  
3.  **Host an interdepartmental Depaving Policy Charrette by Fall 2025**, coordinated by PLACE and CSO. Focus on surfacing interagency barriers, aligning technical standards, and setting shared priorities for implementation workflows.  
4.  **Revise scoring criteria for Measure A and Measure W programs by FY2026** to explicitly reward pavement removal and green retrofits. Ensure that toolkit-aligned projects are advantaged in the selection process and emphasize multiple-benefit outcomes.  
5.  **<comment_start id=kix.33cwy86pazkt>Generate internal 2–5 page policy briefs for each department** to support executive review and inform program design. These should summarize relevant recommendations, anticipated benefits, and operational needs aligned with each agency’s mandate.  
    <comment_end id=kix.33cwy86pazkt>
6.  **<comment_start id=kix.t77t0aou9x1v>Coordinate depaving integration into the state’s Integrated Climate Adaptation and Resiliency Program (ICARP) and into the Chief Sustainability Office’s ICARP-aligned planning efforts**, including watershed strategies and CFMP implementation, to ensure climate funding alignment and program coherence. **The County should also develop a CFMP Implementation and Resilience Assessment (IRA) **as a structured evaluation framework to assess cross-program alignment and ensure that funding, permitting, and technical standards advance long-term resilience goals. Depaving and shade efforts should be explicitly included in this assessment.  
    <comment_end id=kix.t77t0aou9x1v>
7.  **Embed new depaving targets in the 2025 OurCounty Plan** and use them to guide FY2026–2030 budget priorities across capital infrastructure, resilience, and sustainability portfolios. Include depaving targets and pavement removal criteria in future updates to County scorecards and CFMP dashboards, with site-level tracking enabled via GIS.  
8.  **<comment_start id=kix.yp4illcecbsj>Prepare external-facing 2–5 page “Pavement Papers” for each agency**, including maps, site priorities, relevant design standards, and tailored field-level recommendations for agency staff and implementation partners. Issue a targeted insert for LAUSD and small school districts that includes site typologies (e.g., shovel-ready, long-term remediation), soil safety categories, and green design templates that align with physical education standards.<comment_end id=kix.yp4illcecbsj>  
9.  **<comment_start id=kix.usjvioc992n2>Develop and distribute a County Supervisors’ briefing document**, outlining short-, mid-, and long-term program goals, district-specific priorities, and key implementation barriers. Tailor messaging to the political and geographic interests of each Supervisor's district.<comment_end id=kix.usjvioc992n2>
10. **<comment_start id=kix.jeusivriab2j>Create a funding roadmap** that aligns each recommendation with potential sources, including SCWP Education Grants, MWD co-funding, and state bond resources. Include application windows, eligibility thresholds, and strategic bundling options.<comment_end id=kix.jeusivriab2j>

Rather than enforce the 10% landscape area requirement, we recommend the County update commercial retrofit standards to incentivize compact and angled parking conversions. These changes could enable widespread pavement removal and integration of green infrastructure with minimal disruption to site function.
The County should also fund further study to determine how much commercial and industrial parking is actually used vs excess capacity. Industrial warehousing, trucking terminals and parking lots concentrated pavement uses where depaving can scale with appropriate incentives. 
#### **Commercial/Industrial Parcels**
**Finding  
**Commercial and industrial parcels contain tens of thousands of acres of non-core pavement, much of it not required for functional use. 
**Recommendation  
**Commercial and industrial  parcel incentives can target parking lot retrofits. 
#### **6.5.4.4 Residential Parcels**
Single Family residential parcels have the largest total amount of pavement, and multifamily parcels tend to have more pavement per parcel. Incentives for large scale depaving projects can be targeted at larger multifamily properties, while single family incentives may be most economically geared towards small tree wells and DIY projects.

## **<comment_start id=kix.bxn23z4p8uxc>Problem-Solution Framework**
### **Problem: Too Much Pavement (and Why It’s So Hard to Remove)**
Despite support from both governmental and non-governmental sectors, the implementation of depaving initiatives has proven challenging, with limited measurable progress to date. Reflecting on the early years of the Safe, Clean Water Program, Bruce Reznik, Executive Director of L.A. Waterkeeper, acknowledged that “the lack of hardscape removal is definitely troubling, and that jumps out.”
Depaving presents a wide range of interconnected challenges that helps explain why the practice has remained underutilized despite its broad benefits. First, the practice is inherently interdisciplinary, touching on stormwater, transportation, public health, and urban forestry, making coordinated action across siloed agencies difficult. Second, depaving is not yet widely recognized as a go-to solution by either infrastructure managers or community advocates, which limits its inclusion in planning and policy frameworks. Finally, the costs and benefits of depaving are often distributed unevenly: while benefits like ~~cleaner air,~~ reduced flooding, and cooler neighborhoods accrue regionally, the financial and logistical burdens are typically borne at the parcel or block scale.~~ This report, along with its dataset and policy recommendations, aims to address these gaps by elevating depaving as a climate, health, and equity strategy worthy of mainstream consideration.~~ 
### **Solution: Strategic Depaving (through Multi-Sector Coordination)**
Success in this endeavor requires coordination across departments, cities, and agencies. But without a shared framework for addressing interconnected issues like water management, urban heat, traffic, and pedestrian safety, even well-intentioned initiatives often falter. To address these challenges, this report introduces an innovative methodology and dataset to identify pavement burden, including four novel land use [pavement classifications](#heading=h.fk1h29xrd6kp). These classifications aim to define and distinguish between pavement types and ownership, laying the groundwork for more targeted and actionable strategies. 
In a densely built region such as Los Angeles County, removing large swathes of pavement is often unrealistic. Small, distributed interventions, however, while modest on their own, can yield substantial benefits when implemented at scale. Our work is to identify where these opportunities exist and how they can be implemented at scale.
This report thus advances a methodology for implementing modular, site-specific depaving interventions that can be scaled and adapted across diverse site  contexts. <comment_start id=kix.rbr0m7v6u0i3>It first identifies where pavement is located and then speculates where pavement could be removed from key locations<comment_end id=kix.rbr0m7v6u0i3>, prioritizing areas where the greatest environmental and social benefits can be achieved. This approach supports flexibility and adaptability, affording solutions that are both feasible and tailored to the needs of each site and community.
**Strategies  
**This report outlines four practical strategies to support planners, agencies, and advocates in integrating depaving into project design and policy implementation.
1.  **Pavement Mapping and Classification  
    **Use parcel- and right-of-way–level geospatial data to quantify pavement burden and categorize different pavement types and ownership structures across the County.
2.  **Opportunity Identification and Prioritization**
Assess where pavement can be feasibly removed, and prioritize locations based on environmental and social need—factoring in heat exposure, flood risk, canopy gaps, and community vulnerability.
1.  **Scalable Design Typologies  
    **Develop adaptable intervention models tailored to common urban conditions—such as parking lots, sidewalks, medians, and schoolyards—that can be replicated across sites.  
2.  **Policy Integration  
    **Offer  specific recommendations for integrating depaving into County and municipal programs, capital planning, and interdepartmental coordination efforts—including alignment with the CFMP, Safe Clean Water Program, and L.A. County Water Plan.<comment_end id=kix.bxn23z4p8uxc>
Structured to move from foundational data to actionable strategies, the report begins with a needs-based assessment and culminates in policy recommendations aimed at embedding depaving within institutional operations across jurisdictions. Each chapter builds on the last—starting with an analysis of environmental risk factors, mapping pavement distribution, and advancing through adaptive design strategies, heuristic modeling, and cross-sector policy alignment. 
Our hybrid methodology combines rigorous geospatial analysis with planning heuristics and grounded policy insights. It identifies where depaving can yield the greatest environmental and social benefit, and outlines how to realize those opportunities through coordinated agency action. Designed to serve a wide range of audiences—including County departments, local municipalities, architects, engineers, planners, and community advocates—the report offers both site-level detail and scalable, system-level strategies to guide implementation.
