### 6.1 Integration into Countywide Plans and Standards
#### **<comment_start id=kix.a7yaoxarlvfk>6.1.1 Plan Coordination<comment_end id=kix.a7yaoxarlvfk>**
**Finding**: 
Depaving has been identified as a useful strategy to reach the tree canopy goals of the 2024 CMFP, and indeed has been mandated by the October 8 CFMP motion, “Adoption and Equitable Implementation of the Los Angeles County Community Forest Management Plan.” There is now a promising opportunity to integrate specific depaving targets as a standard feature of sustainability plans. <comment_start id=kix.wr7srwgtw6fm>A version of this report’s depaving target, calling for **1,600 acres to be depaved on schools and in public spaces, **is now out for comment, and** **will be included in the upcoming OurCounty Plan, to be published in \_\_\_**. **<comment_end id=kix.wr7srwgtw6fm>
**Recommendation<comment_start id=kix.nrwtgc52hy6i> 1**:<comment_end id=kix.nrwtgc52hy6i>
We recommend the County adopt measurable depaving targets into not only the forthcoming OurCounty Sustainability Plan Update but also the upcoming CFMP, Heat Action Plan, and all sustainability plans produced subsequently. These targets should include both a cumulative acreage goal and disaggregated sub-goals by land use type (e.g. residential, parking lots, schools etc). These metrics should directly support and align with CFMP canopy equity objectives and should prioritize areas with extreme heat, low vegetation cover, flood risk, and vulnerability to climate-related health outcomes. Targets should be embedded at the same level of accountability as existing tree planting and shade canopy metrics. 
To support these planning updates, the County should establish a consistent and authoritative source of land cover classification data. Ensuring that all departments, particularly those managing tree canopy, stormwater, and public health, use the same baseline datasets will be critical for aligning depaving, shade, and heat mitigation goals across planning documents and program metrics.
**Finding:  
**Watershed-scale analysis reveals that industrial pavement within the Lower Los Angeles River, Upper San Gabriel River, and Santa Monica Bay watersheds is both extensive and flood-prone. These priority areas show a clear intersection of impermeable surface area with flood risk, especially in industrial zones.
**Recommendation 2:  
**Integrate depaving of flood-prone industrial parcels into watershed-scale stormwater and groundwater recharge planning under programs such as the SCWP. Prioritize interventions by land use and watershed context to maximize hydrologic benefit and align with regional water resilience goals.
#### **6.1.2 Design Standards / RFPs**
**Finding**: 
<comment_start id=kix.etbe58jtlcr0>Depaving is not currently a required component of green infrastructure design standards or RFPs, nor does depaving appear in the scoring criteria of all grants. While CALGreen voluntary tiers (e.g., A5.106.3 and A5.106.11.1) encourage permeable paving and stormwater mitigation strategies, they stop short of requiring a formal analysis of pavement removal. Furthermore, Los Angeles County’s prevailing design standards, particularly those governing parking lot layout and right-of-way (ROW) dimensions, often limit or complicate the integration of stormwater capture and greening strategies such as planters, bioswales, and tree wells.<comment_end id=kix.etbe58jtlcr0> This report identifies opportunities to revise minimum parking stall dimensions, drive aisle widths, and planter setbacks to better support depaving and green infrastructure retrofits. It also notes that some zoning codes either prohibit or neglect to incentivize designs that allow for integrated vegetation or pervious materials in private lots and public ROWs.
**Recommendation 3**:
Establish a “Depaving First” Standard in All Green Infrastructure and Public Realm Capital ProjectsDesign
A. <comment_start id=kix.ex5tc5xy519p>Adopt a Depaving First Principle<comment_end id=kix.ex5tc5xy519p>Standard in RFPs and Design Guidelines  
All County and municipal design standards and funding solicitations for green infrastructure, mobility, and streetscape projects should incorporate a “Depaving First” principle. This means that depaving must be treated as a foundational consideration, on par with <comment_start id=kix.ug5xl59ccxoc>planting<comment_end id=kix.ug5xl59ccxoc>, drainage, or safety, in both project scoping and design. Requests for Proposals (RFPs), scoring criteria, and agency-specific design manuals (e.g. parking lot and right-of-way design standards) should be revised to explicitly require evaluation of depaving potential as part of project planning.  
B. Implement a Required Depaving Evaluation in Project Development  
Projects that propose changes to paved areas should be required to conduct a “Depaving Evaluation” as an early step in the design process. This evaluation would identify paved surfaces that are unnecessary for safety, access, or programmatic use and prioritize them for removal. Agencies can use the opportunity layers and land use typologies from this report to inform these evaluations. 
C. Update Design Standards to <comment_start id=kix.17me8dsdiqz2>Include<comment_end id=kix.17me8dsdiqz2> Nature-Based Replacements for Pavement  
Once pavement removal areas are identified, updated design standards should offer a menu of climate- and stormwater-aligned NBS interventions to fill in the reclaimed space. This may include bioswales, tree trenches, infiltration planters, habitat zones, shaded pedestrian paths, and community gathering areas. It may also include revising parking lot codes to allow smaller stall dimensions and reduced drive aisle widths where feasible, and encourage angled parking, freeing space for bioswales, planters, and tree wells. The revised design standards can also include modifying ROW and sidewalk design standards to support vegetated curb extensions, reduced curb radii, and modular tree well systems.
 
D. These updates should draw from the depaving strategies developed in this report and be tailored to site conditions (e.g., ROW, parking lots, schoolyards, vacant parcels). The new standards should integrate performance metrics to track benefits related to heat reduction, runoff capture, and canopy expansion.
E. A depaving toolkit to guide implementation, including best practices for retrofits, cost-benefit benchmarks, and species selection tools should be made available to all project partners.
**Finding**
<comment_start id=kix.by4f4vq85jt1>While individual County agencies have adopted various climate and infrastructure goals, they currently lack a structured framework for scaling and sequencing depaving interventions. Without clear implementation pathways, <comment_start id=kix.oowdyc2iv6vn>local jurisdictions<comment_end id=kix.oowdyc2iv6vn> face difficulty prioritizing opportunities and selecting feasible projects. The absence of a decision-making structure impedes the translation of depaving strategies into action. To address this gap, this report developed a three-tiered typology for organizing Depaving Strategies by level of difficulty, ranging from <comment_end id=kix.by4f4vq85jt1>low-cost, tactical interventions to more ambitious site transformations. <comment_start id=kix.by4f4vq85jt1>, especially when municipalities are unfamiliar with depaving or constrained by budget, permitting, or design guidance. <comment_end id=kix.by4f4vq85jt1>
**Recommendation 4**: 
We recommend that the County use the three-tiered typology as a decision-support tool. By organizing depaving strategies by level of difficulty, the County can: (1) identify near-term, low-cost interventions to pilot quickly; (2) pair moderate-level strategies with existing capital projects for efficient integration; and (3) keep the most ambitious site transformations on the radar for long-term planning and funding. Framing Depaving Strategies as a menu of possibilities rather than a prescription allows departments and partners to adopt the options most feasible for their context. This incremental, flexible approach helps the County build early wins, demonstrate progress, and scale up over time.
To address this gap, we propose the following  three-tiered typology for organizing <comment_start id=kix.16bb5ns980s8>the Depaving Strategies by level of difficulty. <comment_end id=kix.16bb5ns980s8>Options range from low-cost, tactical interventions to more ambitious site transformations. Our recommendations intentionally offer a menu of options rather than a single prescriptive blueprint. By showcasing many possible interventions together, partners can identify one or two strategies that feel feasible today, without committing to an all-or-nothing transformation. This “shoot for the stars, reach the moon” approach maximizes flexibility and builds momentum through incremental successes. 
<comment_start id=kix.9smaw7y7x52n>Our first recommendation, to overcome the siloed nature of depaving as it is practiced today, is to empower inter-departmental agency initiatives to coordinate lead depaving efforts, implement pilots and incentive programs, and track results. A good example is the newly-formed Canopy Equity and Depaving (CED) subgroup of the CFMP implementation team<comment_end id=kix.9smaw7y7x52n>.
<comment_start id=kix.l50q84j808sr>We recommendIn what follows, we identify this subgroup, which is co-led by the Department of Public Health’s PLACE program and the CSO, becomes a candidate to bebecome (or act as)as the depaving taskforce charged with implementing this report’s recommendations, but other candidates could also be considered. <comment_end id=kix.l50q84j808sr>
### 6.2 Interdepartmental Coordination & Governance
#### **6.2.1 Agency Roles: Alignment & Accountabili<comment_start id=kix.qmcacr7b3bje>ty<comment_end id=kix.qmcacr7b3bje>**
**Finding**
<comment_start id=kix.9mnp53saxqra>Depaving can advance both traffic safety and stormwater goals, but current funding streams are often siloed and underutilized. <comment_end id=kix.9mnp53saxqra><comment_start id=kix.sgkjxpdlvach>Many of the County’s Vision Zero priority corridors overlap with areas of high pavement burden<comment_end id=kix.sgkjxpdlvach> and stormwater vulnerability. However, roadway redesigns often miss the opportunity to remove excess hardscape. At the same time, SCWP criteria support stormwater-aligned right-of-way retrofits but have not been consistently leveraged to supplement constrained road budgets. Aligning these programs offers a strategic pathway to fund depaving through already existing initiatives.
**Recommendation 5**
The county should leverageconsider Vision Zero projects to , <comment_start id=kix.g704wqvqis08>Safe Clean Water Program (SCWP), and Metropolitan Water District of Southern California (MWD) as potential funding paths to <comment_end id=kix.g704wqvqis08>implementing right-of-way depaving and re-design. Prioritize corridors where safety, stormwater, and climate goals align by layering green infrastructure into traffic calming measures such as curb extensions and sidewalk expansions. Use the opportunity mapping and hotspot analysis in this report to target zones of overlap. Revise SCWP scoring criteria and WASC priorities as needed to better accommodate multimodal, safety-oriented depaving interventions, and ensure that design standards do not inhibit eligibility. Create a depaving task force to coordinate interagency depaving activities. Public Works should gather geospatial data on sidewalk widths, curb cuts, signage, and red zones to inform updated repaving standards and prioritize areas where impervious surfaces can be reduced. Throughout this process, the County should track all forward movement of depaving, both in terms of measuring successful alignment of funding opportunities, and project implementation.    
**Findin<comment_start id=kix.pre5b9njl9ku>g<comment_end id=kix.pre5b9njl9ku>**
Our depaving costs estimates, when run through some typical scenarios for claiming the SCWP tax credit, reveal that it is not sufficient to incentivize most depaving projects. Current SCWP planning tools, including Opportunity Maps for stormwater capture and multiple benefit siting, do not sufficiently account for land transformation potential through depaving or brownfield conversion. Despite alignment with SCWP goals, projects that permanently reduce runoff at the source are largely excluded from prioritization and funding. A forthcoming “Hardscape and Brownfield Transformation Opportunity Study” conducted by the SCWP concludes that “Only 6 of the 101 funded projects will create a new park space or green a school. Rather than creating new green space, most SCWP projects are instead rehabilitating existing parks with stormwater features. Only 30.3 acres of hardscape have been removed by construction projects over Rounds 1-3 (while 27.5 acres of hardscape have been added).” This imbalance limits the County’s ability to achieve long-term stormwater reduction, equitable investment, and resilience through nature-based infrastructure.
**Recommendation 6**: The County should eExplore how SCWP eligibility and prioritization criteria could explicitly reward land transformation through depaving and brownfield conversion. We recommend invite the Safe, Clean Water Program (SCWP) to consider revising its scoring rubric, project criteria, and Opportunity Maps to reflect the long-term stormwater, public health, and access benefits of hardscape removal. SCWP-funded planning tools and Watershed Area Steering Committees (WASCs) Future Watershed Plans should incorporate a “depavingland transformation” metric that evaluates the potential for projects to permanently reduce impervious cover and increase permeable, green surfaces. This includes prioritizing vacant lots, schoolyards, over-paved ROW, and underutilized commercial or industrial parcels where transformation can yield durable runoff reductions.
**Finding:  
A significant number of government-owned parcels are mislabeled as "unspecified"** in existing land use datasets. **School districts** and **the County** control far more pavement than government land use codes suggest. Since publicly controlled land is often easier to access for capital projects, this misclassification poses a missed opportunity to identify high-leverage interventions on land already within the public domain. Without clearer classification, agencies may overlook promising parcels that could support tree planting, stormwater capture, or public space enhancements.
**Recommendation 7:  
The County should conduct a focused re-examination of government-owned parcels currently labeled as “unspecified” in the land use database**. Improving the accuracy of these classifications will surface hidden depaving opportunities and allow for more precise, equity-informed planning across County-owned and agency-managed land. This should be integrated into future dataset updates and coordinated with relevant departments such as The Assessor’s Office, Planning, Public Works, and Parks.
**Finding**
**Depaveable areas within the public right-of-way (ROW) present a high-leverage opportunity to improve shade cover**, particularly along residential and commercial streets heavily used for walking, biking, and transit. Because these corridors serve as active transportation routes, increasing tree well planting in the ROW can directly address extreme heat exposure and equity gaps in canopy coverage, especially in neighborhoods with limited access to cooling infrastructure.
**Recommendation 8**
<comment_start id=kix.b0cyqx7mc6qa>Prioritize depaving within the ROW to expand tree well planting on pedestrian and transit corridors. County departments and local jurisdictions should proactively identify segments with wide sidewalks, excess shoulder space, or oversized medians for strategic pavement removal and canopy enhancement. These efforts should be integrated into climate adaptation and active transportation plans to maximize co-benefits and target areas of highest vulnerability.<comment_end id=kix.b0cyqx7mc6qa>
**<comment_start id=kix.hra40pqafaz2>Finding**:
We established a 5 year target for removing \>600 acres of pavement from school properties and \>10000 from residential and commercial parcels.
**Recommendation 9**:
To maintain accountability, depaving projects should seek to meet the targets set by the governing agencies.<comment_end id=kix.hra40pqafaz2>
### 6.3 Pilot Projects: 
#### **County Pilot Projects**
**Finding**:
The An October 2024 motion to implement the CFMP includeds the a directive to “Direct the CSO, in collaboration with the Directors of DPW, Parks and Recreation (DPR), and other relevant Departments to seek funding for and implement pilot depaving projects on County property, including the right-of-way, in high canopy need communities, and consider opportunities to integrate trees into conceptual project designs associated with Vision Zero and other community improvement initiatives.” 
**Recommendation 9**:
The County should implement the directive of the use the funds allocated by the CFMP motion byto selecting a series of pilot depaving projects on County land based on the priority hotspots identified in this analysis. Those projects will present an opportunity to experiment with the range of depaving strategies outlined in this report. The lLessons learned from thoese pilot projects in the first year of their implementation will can then be used to develop guidelines for a private parcel incentive program (see section 6.4). 
Site locations could include ROW projects overseen by Public Works, but also any number of facilities managed by departments including ISD, Courts, Libraries, LACDA, DHS, Animal Control, Sheriff, LACoFD, etc. To produce greater visibility, reach, and community buy-in, possible sites could also include opportunities on public property in leading cities such as the City of L.A. 
#### **Vacant Parcels**
**Finding**
Vacant parcels in Los Angeles County contain over 11,000 acres of potentially unused pavement, representing a major opportunity for removal. While residential and commercial parcels contribute the most in total, industrial and commercial vacant parcels are far more pavement-intensive, with average impervious cover exceeding 23% and 30%, respectively.
**Recommendation 10**
Because vacant parcels can be depaved without disrupting ongoing activities, we recommend incentives and permitting waivers for vacant parcel depaving, possibly embedding those properties under County control in the pilot program.
#### **<comment_start id=kix.4wtfks9sgsdk>Commercial & Private Parcels<comment_end id=kix.4wtfks9sgsdk>**
### 6.4 Incentives Program
#### **6.4.2 Residential and Commercial Program **
**Finding**
Residential and commercial properties together account for the majority of pavement in Los Angeles County, with residential parcels alone comprising 41.7% and commercial parcels 14.6% of total parcel-based pavement area. Much of these tens of thousands of acres of pavement are required for functional use. We have also shown that multifamily parcels tend to have more pavement per parcel than single family homes. 
But as our cost analysis demonstrates, without targeted incentives, private property owners have little financial motivation to remove pavement, especially where aesthetic or functional norms prioritize hardscape. This lack of incentives poses a major barrier to achieving countywide transformation goals and calls for a dedicated program structure, supported by cost data, design tools, and pilot project lessons. Reconfiguring parking layouts and enforcing landscape requirements could unlock thousands of acres for depaving and tree planting without sacrificing utility.
**Recommendation 11**:
One year into the CFMP-funded pilot project described above (6.3), and building on lessons learned from those projects, the depaving task force should then assist agencies in dedicating funds from existing programs and from grants towards an incentive program for depaving private property. These efforts will be leveraged by a growing corpus of toolkits, design guides, data on impacts and co-benefits, as well as validated cost estimates.  
Commercial parcel incentives could target parking lot retrofits. Rather than enforce the 10% landscape area requirement, we recommend the County update commercial retrofit standards to incentivize compact and angled parking conversions, among a range of other options laid out in Chapter 5. These changes could enable widespread pavement removal and integration of green infrastructure with minimal disruption to site function.
As for residential properties, incentives for large-scale depaving projects could be targeted at larger multifamily residential properties, while single family incentives may be most economically geared towards small tree wells and facilitating more modest DIY projects. 
Finally, the County should fund further study to determine how much commercial parking is actually used versus how much represents excess capacity. Industrial warehousing, trucking terminals, and large parking lots concentrate pavement in ways that present scalable depaving opportunities if paired with appropriate incentives.
#### **6.4.3 Industrial Program **
**Finding**
Industrial parcels used for parking, trucking terminals, and storage yards have the highest average pavement intensity of any land use in Los Angeles County. These high-intensity sites contribute disproportionately to stormwater runoff, urban heat, and flood vulnerability, especially in communities already facing stacked environmental burdens. Although these parcels are typically in active use, their intensity makes them strategic long-term targets for depaving, particularly in freight corridors and legacy industrial zones.
**Recommendation 12**
Integrate high-intensity industrial parcels, such as trucking terminals, storage yards, and parking lots, into long-term depaving and climate resilience strategies. Jurisdictions should assess opportunities to reduce impervious area over time through freight transition planning, incentive structures, and land use reforms that encourage permeable alternatives or redevelopment aligned with stormwater and heat mitigation goals.
#### **6.4.4 Toolkit for Property O<comment_start id=kix.8njmbx4yr2we>wners<comment_end id=kix.8njmbx4yr2we>**
**Finding**
Many depaving opportunities lie on private property, yet most property owners lack clear, accessible guidance on how to initiate and implement depaving projects. The absence of standardized design templates, permitting pathways, and cost-benefit data creates a barrier to action, especially for small- and medium-scale projects.
**Recommendation 13**
We recommend the County fund the writing of depaving toolkits for property owners. Such guides should outline their available adaptation options, as well as inform them about design standards, permitting requirements and process, the logical flow of project execution, and access to other resources available from the taskforce. 
### 6.5 Schools 
**Finding**
Schools represent a major opportunity for pavement removal, with 9,198 acres of paved area across public school district and University owned parcels in Los Angeles County. This is  approximately 24% of all pavement on public parcels in Los Angeles County.  Across all school campus parcels public and private, there is 14,663 acres of pavement.
**Recommendation 14**
Though outside of County jurisdiction, school districts could benefit from County support in convening a cross-jurisdictional accountability roundtable, inviting participation from LAUSD, Los Angeles County Office of Education (LACOE), and other relevant agencies, to support implementation of school greening goals and improve interagency coordination. The roundtable could include legal, facilities, and program staff to establish shared expectations, clarify liability thresholds, and create conflict resolution protocols that reduce procedural delays.
**Finding**
LAUSD has allocated approximately $1.2 billion in potential greening funds, but it is not for depaving or nature-based play. There is a need to balance pavement needs for certain activities and sports but significant additional pavement.
**Recommendation 15:** 
  - The County and its partners should encourage LAUSD and other school districts to allocate a defined portion of bond-funded greening resources to Depaving and nature-based play areas. Schools should utilize this data to prioritize spending in the areas of most need. 
  - The County should work with LAUSD, LACOE, and state education agencies to amend PE standards so that trees, permeable surfaces, and natural play spaces can count toward active use requirements. 
  - To facilitate uptake, model site plans and design guidelines should be developed showing how multi-benefit schoolyards can satisfy stormwater, shade, and physical activity goals simultaneously.
**Finding**
This assessment calculated the total pavement on school properties, but did not attempt to discern how much of that pavement was needed for ball courts and other school specific pavement needs. 
**Recommendation 16:**
Future assessments should look into greater detail about specific uses of pavement on school parcels to obtain an estimate of how much pavement could be removed. 
### 6.6 Develop Implementation Plans
The depave taskforce should develop and implement a goal setting framework, set targets, and establish/evaluate indicators. These should be formulated into specific deliverables to be provided to depave partners. The taskforce should also develop  a Monitoring/Evaluation/Adaptation plan where assessment is redone to see change analysis for 2016, 2020, 2023 and every three years moving forward. A full cost benefit analysis that includes co-benefits and downstream health impacts should be conducted to inform future policy decisions. The taskforce should open their corpus to other delve organizations in Portland, Chicago, and the Depaving Network. 
**Finding**:
These data, and especially our tiered mapping of stacked needs, provides a clear framework for prioritizing near-term depaving efforts. They enable policymakers to direct investments toward locations with the highest multi-risk burdens and immediate outreach potential. The approach also facilitates scalable site selection by aligning data-driven need with public control, community partnerships, and visible public benefit.
**Recommendation 17**:
We therefore recommend that the County and its partners target their work in these highest needs assessment areas as identified in chapter 2.
**Finding:  
Pavement burden must be understood through both total coverage and intensity.** While single-family residential parcels account for the largest total amount of residential pavement countywide, this is due to their sheer number. When considering intensity, the proportion of each parcel that is paved, multifamily and commercial lots often bear a greater burden, with up to 23% of multifamily parcels paved on average compared to 13% for single-family. This dual perspective reveals not only where the majority of pavement exists, but also where residents are disproportionately affected by high pavement coverage relative to the size of their lot.
**Recommendation 18:  
Incorporate both total and intensity-based metrics when identifying depaving priorities**. Total pavement area helps target large-scale interventions, while intensity highlights areas where residents experience concentrated environmental burdens. Prioritizing high-intensity sites, particularly in multifamily and commercial zones, can advance equity by focusing on communities facing the most acute impacts of heat, flooding, and lack of green space. Future tools and policy frameworks should integrate both perspectives to guide nuanced, effective intervention strategies.
### 6.7 Develop Depaving Consortium/Working Groups
The depave taskforce should provide Technical Assistance to communities that want to depave. This should include support for government officials, professionals, individuals and communities who want depave. <comment_start id=kix.x9s6qvqxwp0b>The taskforce should work to **<comment_end id=kix.x9s6qvqxwp0b>Increase Implementation** **Capacity **with public private partnerships, to leverage  depaving material handlers, contractors and small businesses. This can facilitate a network to address logistics, material handling, soil amendments  and tree planting groups. A chain of custody process should be implemented to assure pavement grinding facilities don't provide additional impact to community health, and alternative methods of making use of removed pavement should be researched and disseminated.
### 6.8 Adaptive Managemen<comment_start id=kix.olk9gtdybp87>t<comment_end id=kix.olk9gtdybp87>
*For detailed recommendations on future  monitoring, evaluation and adaptation research please see section 5.7.3. For future research into depaving assessment methodologies, please refer to section 7.5. *
**<comment_start id=kix.1de247iaj0xl>Finding:**
Depaving at the County scale requires solving complicated problems across agencies, disciplines, and partnerships. This can most effectively be implemented by continuously accruing wisdom based on experience as the process unfolds.<comment_end id=kix.1de247iaj0xl>
**Recommendation:**
We recommend that depaving Los Angeles be implemented as an adaptive management cycle. Depaving pilot projects can be designed and modeled, then implemented with community support, and finally monitored to determine their impact on heat, stormwater, tree canopy, and community health outcomes. These monitoring results can be evaluated and used to determine ROIs and iterative design improvements, and then fed into the next cycle of design, on a larger set of projects. This is an iterative, applied,  scientific approach to built environment transformation. The depaving taskforce can steward this process, maintaining a corpus of longitudinal data on depaving impacts, obstacles, and lessons learned that can be used to support future generations of depavers. 
**Finding**: 
This report does not establish what pavement can specifically be removed or not. To do so would require more detailed geospatial analysis, on-the-ground site surveys, community outreach, and careful program management. 
**Recommendation**:
We recommend that depaving activities pool resources across partnerships to maintain a coordinated approach so that, as higher quality data is generated, it can be made available to others. We further recommend that these more detailed assessments be done in areas identified as highest impact in this report

To ensure that new green spaces meet multiple policy goals, the County should also explore partnership with the Department of Public Health to co-develop schoolyard greening guidelines that support both physical education requirements and urban cooling objectives. 
These guidelines should incorporate advanced thermal comfort metrics—such as Mean Radiant Temperature (MRT) and Universal Thermal Climate Index (UTCI)—to prioritize interventions in park-poor or heat-vulnerable areas. 
Our recommendations intentionally offer a menu of options rather than a single prescriptive blueprint. By showcasing many possible interventions together, partners can identify one or two strategies that feel feasible today, without committing to an all-or-nothing transformation. This “shoot for the stars, reach the moon” approach maximizes flexibility and builds momentum through incremental successes.

***Key findings to add ***
**1. Depaving needs to be institutionalized**
  - Finding: Despite widespread alignment with County goals, depaving is not yet a required element in design standards, RFPs, or planning frameworks.  
  - Recommendation: Adopt a “Depaving First” standard across County projects. Require a “Depaving Evaluation” in all RFPs and update design standards to pair depaving with nature-based solutions (e.g., tree wells, bioswales, green corridors).
**2. Vacant and Public Parcels Offer Quick Wins**
  - Finding: Vacant parcels alone contain over 11,000 acres of pavement, while government and school-owned parcels offer high-leverage opportunities under public control.  
  - Recommendation: Pilot depaving projects on public land as an early intervention opportunity, in alignment with the motion to do depaving pilot projects, in the CFMP plan approved by the Board of Supervisors in Fall 2024. 
  - Incentivize and streamline vacant parcel depaving in high needs areas through permitting waivers and embedded pilots.
**3. Schools Are Critical to Climate Resilience**
  - Finding: School Campuses contain \> 14000 acres of pavement  
  - Recommendation: Partner with LAUSD, LACOE, and other L.A. school districts to allocate greening funds toward depaving and nature-based play amenities to create greener, cooler campuses.
**4. Residential, Commercial, and Industrial Sites Require Incentives**
  - Finding: Residential and commercial parcels account for the majority of parcel-based pavement. Yet private owners lack sufficient incentives to depave, especially where excess parking or hardscape is the norm.  
  - Recommendation: Develop a  depaving incentive program to encourage retrofits of private properties. Target multifamily properties, parking lots, and high-intensity industrial sites.
**5. High-Need “Hotspot” Areas Should Be Prioritized for Depaving**
  - Finding: More than 1,500 acres of pavement are in areas simultaneously experiencing extreme heat, flood risk, pavement burden, and low tree canopy. 
  - Finding: According to the SB535 definition of Disadvantaged Communities, they contain 147,487 acres of total pavement (approximately half of the total), of which 65,905 acres are thought to not be in use as roads or sidewalks, or required for parking.  
  - Recommendation: Direct early investments and pilot programs toward these stacked-needs hotspots and otherwise disadvantaged areas, including Vision Zero corridors and schools in high-vulnerability zones.
**6. Design Standards & Planning Must Catch Up**
  - Finding: Current standards for parking dimensions, drive aisle widths, and ROW layout limit space for greening.  
  - Recommendation: Update codes to support compact/angled parking, reduce curb radii, and enable modular tree well systems, freeing space for trees, infiltration, and cooling infrastructure.
7\. Depaving Should Be Tracked, Funded & Coordinated
  - Finding: Depaving lacks a coordinated governance structure and is underleveraged in County programs like SCWP and Vision Zero.  
  - Recommendation: Empower the CFMP Canopy Equity and Depaving (CED) subgroup to lead implementation, track progress, and align funding across agencies. Revise SCWP and other grant scoring criteria to prioritize land transformation through depaving.

##### **Patio Conversion (from 5.3.2.4 Activity Space Strategies)**
Our pavement analysis indicates that of the total of 71,327 acres of pavement on residential parcels, we estimate that approximately 26,587 acres is required for parking. What remains (after accounting for some parcels not having sufficient parking)  is 44,097 acres of pavement that is likely to be patios, walkways and driveways. On average, there is 1037 square feet on each of the 1,851,856 residential parcels, and so if we cut a single 6 foot by 6 foot tree will in each of these (amounting to under 4% of the total average patio area) in each of these patios, it would result in a total of **1,530 acres** of depaving. 
**5.6.2.1 Parks Heuristic**
The Los Angeles Countywide Parks and Openspace Dataset  says that there are 3012 parks in the county. Together, these have 5,630 acres of pavement, and are on average 8.4% covered in pavement, however, some parks have up to 24% of their area covered with pavement, and indeed there are 1015 parks that have more than 8.4% pavement coverage. If we removed enough pavement to get all of those down to 8.4%, it would require removing  **892 acres** of pavement.
**5.6.2.2 Schools Heuristic**
  - We calculated pavement coverage for all schools in the County, along with their extreme heat exposure, flood risk and canopy coverage, and will make this data available for school partners.
  - The 3,179 School campuses in Los Angeles County, which serve \>2 million students, contain approximately **14,663 acres** of pavement, with the average school campus being 28% covered in pavement. However, some have much higher pavement coverage.
  - If all of the school campuses with above average pavement coverage were brought down to the average, it would require removing **3,107 acres** of pavement.
Pavement is most concentrated in three land use categories: **Residential**, **Industrial**, and **Government**.
## **Overview**
We summarize insights from each of the previous chapters, recap of our findings: the needs, the pavement assessment, the land use analysis heuristics used to develop non-core pavement area, and finally possible depaving strategies and solutions. 
In these findings we then analyze the Needs Assessment against the possible solutions to help set targets. We also evaluate at both local, regional and national benchmarks and tart. Chapters and provide recommendations for policies, programs, additional needs and Next Steps.
This Chapter begins to move from an Assessment, which was the scope to an actual plan and implementation Strategy.
## **Insights**
  - Pavement is most concentrated in three land use categories: **Residential**, **Industrial**, and **Government**.
  - ~~**Single-family homes**~~~~ contain the majority of residential pavement by acreage, but multi-family parcels are more pavement-intensive.~~
  - ~~**Industrial truck yards**~~~~ and ~~~~**parking lots**~~~~ are more paved, proportionally, than warehouses.~~
  - ~~Ownership analysis reveals ~~~~**school districts**~~~~ and ~~~~**the County**~~~~ control far more pavement than government land use codes suggest.~~
  - ~~Many ~~~~**vacant parcels**~~~~ in commercial and industrial zones are heavily paved, offering low-conflict depaving opportunities.~~
  - ~~These insights support a targeted approach to prioritizing both ~~~~**high-acreage**~~~~ and ~~~~**high-feasibility depaving sites**~~~~.~~
  - Many **schools**, burdened by excessive pavement and low canopy cover, are prone to heat and flood risk. Our rankings can be used to prioritize implementation of this report’s school-specific depaving strategies.  
  - **Watersheds** can use our data to identify high-volume sites to meet their stormwater capture goals.

## **Meeting Goals**
​​**Goals**
This report treats **goals** as broad ambitions that describe the purpose and direction of the Depave initiative, and **targets** as specific, measurable milestones that mark progress toward those ambitions. The overarching goal is to use depaving as a tool to address interconnected challenges—urban heat island effects, tree canopy disparities, stormwater management, and environmental health inequities, that are all addressed in their own plans, and all note paving area as an issue. By removing unnecessary pavement, we can create conditions that support tree planting, reduce localized flooding, improve infiltration, and contribute to healthier, more resilient communities.
### Meeting Goals
### Targets
Setting meaningful targets is an essential part of goal setting. Targets define the scale and pace of change we aim to achieve, and they should be rooted in careful research, benchmarking, and community engagement. In developing our initial targets, we drew on relevant local and national benchmarks, existing city and county plans, and available datasets. We also reviewed target-setting approaches from other jurisdictions to understand what has been considered achievable elsewhere.
The targets presented here are a **first pass**—produced in response to the needs of the OurCounty Plan. They are ambitious and should be refined over time through additional data analysis in a more robust planning process, implementation plan, community input. We recommend that these targets be re-evaluated and “ground-truthed” in future planning cycles, and that goals and adaptation of the plan being set at 3 year cycles (when the county collects data) 
Our intent is for this framework to be adaptable and useful to a range of stakeholders, from County departments to community-based organizations. By aligning depaving targets with broader environmental, health, and equity goals—and by supporting them with clear policies and implementation strategies—we can ensure that the work is both impactful and durable.

## **ARLA OUTLINE (copy-pasted below and linked [here](https://docs.google.com/document/d/10vzoruYpODd5I7YTDpm-JQCzi5bPcCC9aWqXD0yRXHQ/edit?tab=t.0))**
**DRAFT - policy recommendations priority order**
In our spatial analysis of over two million county parcels, we identified key distribution, intensity, and ownership patterns to guide pavement removal strategies. The largest total acreage of pavement is concentrated in three land use categories: Residential, Industrial, and Government. Building on those insights, this chapter lays out an initial policy framework to begin institutionalizing pavement removal as a tool for climate resilience, public health, and equitable infrastructural changes across Los Angeles County. 
We recommend both agency-specific reforms and cross-cutting systems change, covering planning standards, funding programs, governance structures, a County-led pilot implementation program as well as a County-led depaving incentive program. Our hope is to embed a depaving-first principle in County infrastructure strategy. Building on spatial analysis, interdepartmental interviews, and parallel County initiatives (e.g., Community Forest Management Plan, the Vision Zero Action Plan, the forthcoming Heat Action Plan, and Safe, Clean Water Program), our recommendations are organized both by thematic issue area and by agency responsibility. 
The County’s different land uses and ownership structures correspond to different levers for action. Government-owned parcels often represent "low-hanging fruit," as agencies can directly implement changes on land under their control. Residential parcels require incentives and supportive programming to encourage voluntary retrofits. Meanwhile, industrial and commercial properties present opportunities for native landscaping and tree planting, especially in light of state and local restrictions on using potable water to irrigate non-functional turf. These distinctions shape the targeted strategies that follow in this chapter.

1.  Design standards/RFPs
    1.  Depaving First Standard - with both RFPs and design standards - as a principle
    2.  Implemented via: RFP-Depaving Evaluation (what can come out)
    3.   + Design standards should be updated to include what NBS can go back in (updating design standards with options to replace pavement) - and can refer to our depaving strategies to develop it
**Finding**: 
Though depaving is increasingly admired as a strategy, there is a growing opportunity to embed depaving as a default metric in both RFPs and design standards  
**Recommendation**:
All new public projects should adopt a “depaving first” standard, where pavement removal is the default starting point unless infeasibility can be demonstrated. L.A. County should require a “Depaving Evaluation” section in all RFPs for design and capital improvement projects. This should apply to all Public Works, park, and civic campus retrofits. While CALGreen voluntary tiers (e.g., A5.106.3 and A5.106.11.1) encourage permeable paving and stormwater mitigation strategies, they stop short of requiring a formal analysis of pavement removal. Moreover, that depaving should always be paired with clearly defined best management practices (BMPs) addressing stormwater capture, tree canopy gaps, or flood risk to ensure that pavement removal advances broader environmental and public health goals.
LA County Public Works should establish the “depaving-first” standard in updates to the Complete Streets Manual and Green Street specifications. In some cases, this could mean cutting into paved areas like sidewalks or medians to create space for tree wells (ideally 6x6 feet in planting area), bioswales, or stormwater cutouts. In others, it may involve reducing overbuilt pavement to lessen impervious surface area. Public Works should gather geospatial data on sidewalk widths, curb cuts, signage, and red zones to inform updated repaving standards and prioritize areas where impervious surfaces can be reduced. 
Design standards that abide by the depaving-first principle should also be updated to include what nature-based solutions can replace the pavement that has been removed. See our depaving strategies for more details, as well

**UPDATED Depave Policy Recs from **[**slides**](https://docs.google.com/presentation/d/12NN4bhB6-vY8hYK9LpO7_hZ3wJ6nuYtALojUt6xFG1A/edit?slide=id.g362eab21054_0_23#slide=id.g362eab21054_0_23)
1\.** Integrate Depaving into County Plans (via targets) **
  - Embed **quantifiable depaving targets** in the OurCounty Plan, Climate Action Plan, Heat Action Plan, and future plans as they are developed.
**Our Approach:** A data-driven analysis to identify high-impact pavement removal opportunities in communities that need it most.
**How We Calculated the Target**
**1. Commercial & Residential Target (10,485 Acres of pavement removal)**
  - **Analyze:** We assessed every parcel's current land cover and required parking area to determine its "landscape percentage."
  - **Set Goals:** We set targets for parcels that fell below a desired landscape percentage, while still retaining pavement needed to meet parking requirements:
      - **Residential:** 40% Landscaped Area
      - **Commercial:** 10% Landscaped Area
  - **Prioritize:** The analysis was focused *exclusively* on parcels within high-need areas (top 20% for heat or flood risk, or bottom 20% for tree canopy).
**2. Schools Target (641 Acres of pavement removal)**
  - This target was calculated by using the LAUSD Green Schoolyards plan as a benchmark and extrapolating the average pavement removal per school to all schools county-wide.
Final Depaving Target:** 11,126 Acres**
  - **Residential: 9,297 acres**
  - **Commercial:** **1,188 acres**
  - **Schools:** **641 acres**
Note that the draft OurCounty Sustainability Plan currently being circulated for public comment includes a depaving target of **1600 acres for public property and schools** (as a direct result of this work). 
But we would still like to see more ambitious targets akin to those listed above.
2\.** **Adopt a **Depaving-First Standard** to green infrastructure improvements.
  - Require a **“Depaving Evaluation”** in all County RFPs and capital project templates.
  - **Update design manuals** to prioritize tree wells, bioswales, and stormwater BMPs wherever hardscape is removed.
  - **Encourage integrated block-scale strategies**, such as reducing travel lanes to create planted medians, reconfiguring parking lots with tree islands and bioswales, and adding hedgerows or vegetated buffers along residential frontages, to multiply benefits across parcels and right-of-way.
3\. Empower the **Canopy Equity and Depaving (CED)** subgroup of the CFMP implementation team to **lead depaving efforts**, **coordinate across agencies**, and **track progress**.
*(CED, *a new group created by the County to work on agency coordination,* is co-led by the Department of Public Health PLACE program and the Chief Sustainability Office)*
**The County should first develop and** **launch a *****Depaving Pilot Program***** **to meet canopy goals of the 2024 CFMP motion** **
  - Allocate initial funds to pilot depaving projects on **Public Parcels** and in the **ROW**.
One year into the pilot program, and incorporating lessons learned, **the County should** **then** **launch a *****Depaving Incentive Program***** for **private properties 
  - The incentive program could be funded with a combination of funds from **existing funding streams**: SCWP, Vision Zero, Measure A e.g., as well as grant opportunities such as the Prop 4 Climate Bond 
The Incentive Program should offer design kits for tiered retrofits options ranging from easiest to most ambitious
  - **Commercial, residential lots, and vacant parcels** options range in difficulty from 
      - tactical interventions like parklets, sidewalk bioswales, and vacant parcel greening
      - to intermediate-scale conversions such as compact parking redesigns, schoolyard depaving, and ROW tree wells
      - to transformative projects like full greenway redevelopments and major parking lot reconfigurations.
**5. Expand Schoolyard Greening**
  - Support implementation of LAUSD’s 30% greening target through **interagency coordination**, **technical toolkits**, and **soil safety classification frameworks**.
  - **Toolkits** provide assistance with **site assessment**, **design guidelines**, **permitting pathways**, **O\&M strategies**, and guidance for securing **future funding**. 
  - Align schoolyard design with both **educational** and **climate** goals by integrating depaving, tree canopy, and permeable play surfaces.

### 6.1.1 Introduction
~~In our spatial analysis of over two million county parcels, we identified key distribution, intensity, and ownership patterns to guide pavement removal strategies. The largest total acreage of pavement is concentrated in three land use categories: Residential, Industrial, and Government. Building on those insights, this chapter lays out an initial policy framework to begin institutionalizing pavement removal as a tool for climate resilience, public health, and equitable infrastructural changes across Los Angeles County. ~~
~~We recommend both agency-specific reforms and cross-cutting systems change, covering planning standards, funding programs, governance structures, a County-led pilot implementation program as well as a County-led depaving incentive program. Our hope is to embed a depaving-first principle in County infrastructure strategy. Building on spatial analysis, interdepartmental interviews, and parallel County initiatives (e.g., Community Forest Management Plan, the Vision Zero Action Plan, the forthcoming Heat Action Plan, and Safe, Clean Water Program), our recommendations are organized both by thematic issue area and by agency responsibility. ~~
~~The County’s different land uses and ownership structures correspond to different levers for action. Government-owned parcels often represent "low-hanging fruit," as agencies can directly implement changes on land under their control. Residential parcels require incentives and supportive programming to encourage voluntary retrofits. Meanwhile, industrial and commercial properties present opportunities for native landscaping and tree planting, especially in light of state and local restrictions on using potable water to irrigate non-functional turf. These distinctions shape the targeted strategies that follow in this chapter.~~
-----
### 6.1.2 Integration into Countywide Plans and Standards
### **A. Sustainability Documents**
**Challenge:** Despite strong alignment between depaving, tree planting, and heat mitigation, there is no overarching depaving target embedded in L.A. County’s major planning frameworks. Current plans—including the OurCounty Sustainability Plan, Climate Action Plan, and forthcoming Heat Action Plan—reference tree canopy goals but have only recently begun implementing quantifiable pavement removal goals. The CFMP also stops short of defining specific acreage or land use types for depaving.
**Recommendation:** The County should integrate measurable depaving targets into its next updates to the OurCounty Plan, CFMP, and Heat Action Plan. These targets should include both a cumulative acreage goal and disaggregated sub-goals by land use type (e.g., schoolyards, sidewalks, parking lots, parks). These metrics should directly support and align with CFMP canopy equity objectives and should prioritize areas with extreme heat, low vegetation cover, flood risk, and vulnerability to climate-related health outcomes. Targets should be embedded at the same level of accountability as existing tree planting and shade canopy metrics. This report took the extra step to suggest targets for the forthcoming OurCounty sustainability plan which are included in Chapter 7 (sub-section 3.4).  
To support these planning updates, the County should establish a consistent and authoritative source of land cover classification data. Going forward, the County should provide a consistent and authoritative source for this data. ~~the SCWP / Stormwater Division is expected to serve this role. ~~Ensuring that all departments—particularly those managing tree canopy, stormwater, and public health—use the same baseline datasets will be critical for aligning depaving, shade, and heat mitigation goals across planning documents and program metrics.
-----
### **B. Design Standards and RFP Language**
**Challenge:** Across departments, County-issued RFPs and capital project templates rarely require hardscape removal as a default consideration. Without model language or technical criteria for depaving, agencies tend to default to existing pavement footprints—missing key opportunities for green retrofits, bioswales, or tree wells.
**Recommendation:** L.A. County should require a “Depaving Evaluation” section in all RFPs for design and capital improvement projects. This should apply to all Public Works, schoolyard, park, and civic campus retrofits. ~~While CALGreen voluntary tiers (e.g., A5.106.3 and A5.106.11.1) encourage permeable paving and stormwater mitigation strategies, they stop short of requiring a formal analysis of pavement removal. A local “Depaving Evaluation” would build on this foundation, helping institutionalize a stronger standard.~~
In addition to depaving analysis, all project RFPs and design processes should include site-specific environmental benchmarking to assess baseline conditions such as stormwater runoff potential, tree canopy coverage, and urban heat metrics. These benchmarks will help agencies prioritize interventions that deliver the greatest co-benefits in terms of heat reduction, water quality, public health, and equity. Where appropriate, depaving can also support pollinator-friendly habitat, urban food production, and educational or community stewardship opportunities—especially in school, park, and residential settings. Design standards should be flexible enough to accommodate these context-sensitive co-benefits.
~~LA County Public Works should update the Complete Streets Manual and Green Street specifications. In some cases, this could mean cutting into paved areas like sidewalks or medians to create space for tree wells (ideally 6x6 feet in planting area), bioswales, or stormwater cutouts. In others, it may involve reducing overbuilt pavement to lessen impervious surface area. Standards should also provide visual guidance for minimizing impervious surfaces especially in school and civic settings.~~
~~In addition to site-level greening, the County should promote integrated block-scale strategies that coordinate right-of-way and adjacent parcel interventions for maximum benefit. Examples include~~ reducing travel lanes to create planted medians, ~~reconfiguring parking lots with tree islands and bioswales, and installing hedgerows or vegetated buffers along residential or school frontages. These approaches support neighborhood-scale shade, reduce urban heat, improve pedestrian safety, and enhance stormwater performance. By incorporating such block-scale strategies into standard design manuals and RFP requirements, the County can amplify climate resilience and equity outcomes beyond isolated sites.~~
Current utility clearance requirements should also be revisited. Existing code often discourages the use of small-scale trees or shrubs near infrastructure such as street light poles, thus limiting canopy continuity. Allowing closer spacing for appropriate species would support more biodiverse plantings and more flexible use of constrained public space.
Public Works should gather geospatial data on sidewalk widths, curb cuts, signage, and red zones to inform updated repaving standards and prioritize areas where impervious surfaces can be reduced. Ideally, all new public projects should adopt a “depaving first” standard, where pavement removal is the default starting point unless infeasibility can be demonstrated. Moreover, that depaving should always be paired with clearly defined best management practices (BMPs) addressing stormwater capture, tree canopy gaps, or flood risk to ensure that pavement removal advances broader environmental and public health goals.

### **B. Soil Contamination Risk and Liability**
**Challenge:** Fear of soil contamination and liability exposure deters depaving at many public sites—particularly schools and campuses in underserved areas. While this concern is often most visible at LAUSD, it is shared across agencies and jurisdictions. The lack of a standardized environmental review framework for compacted or contaminated soils creates uncertainty and discourages early-stage planning for depaving and greening projects.
**Recommendation:** Los Angeles County should partner with the California Department of Toxic Substances Control (DTSC) to develop a standardized risk classification matrix for public sites, including schools, parks, and civic campuses. This matrix would help indicate which properties are eligible for shallow versus deep interventions and clarify what forms of due diligence or mitigation are required. In parallel, the County should support development of a technical toolkit to help agencies and community partners navigate soil compaction, contamination, and permitting challenges. Long-term efforts could include model legislation that provides limited indemnity under defined scenarios, reducing liability fears that currently stall depaving efforts.
### ~~**C. Bond Funding and Physical Education (PE) Standards**~~
~~**Challenge:**~~~~ While LAUSD has access to approximately $1.2 billion in potential greening funds, there is no binding requirement that any portion be used for depaving or nature-based play. In addition, PE standards often restrict the types of surfaces and layouts that qualify as active use space, making it harder to implement multi-benefit green designs.~~
~~**Recommendation:**~~~~ The County and its partners should encourage LAUSD and other school districts to allocate a defined portion of bond-funded greening resources to depaving and nature-based play areas, either through internal policy commitments or through criteria attached to future funding disbursements. In parallel, the County should work with LAUSD, LACOE, and state education agencies to amend PE standards so that trees, permeable surfaces, and natural play spaces can count toward active use requirements. ~~
~~To ensure that new green spaces meet multiple policy goals, the County should also explore partnership with the Department of Public Health to co-develop schoolyard greening guidelines that support both physical education requirements and urban cooling objectives. These guidelines should incorporate advanced thermal comfort metrics—such as Mean Radiant Temperature (MRT) and Universal Thermal Climate Index (UTCI)—to prioritize interventions in park-poor or heat-vulnerable areas. To facilitate uptake, model site plans and design guidelines should be developed showing how multi-benefit schoolyards can satisfy stormwater, shade, and physical activity goals simultaneously.~~
### **D. Maintenance and ADA Compliance**
**Challenge:** Unresolved operations and maintenance (O\&M) responsibilities and Americans with Disabilities Act (ADA) compliance concerns—such as tree root conflicts or emergency medical services (EMS) vehicle access—frequently block implementation of greening projects on school campuses, even after funding has been secured. While these challenges are especially acute in Los Angeles Unified School District (LAUSD) settings, they affect public infrastructure projects more broadly and merit cross-agency coordination.
**Recommendation:** Pilot a shared O\&M model for school campuses, with roles divided between LAUSD, the Los Angeles Sanitation and Environment Department (LASAN), and non-governmental organization (NGO) partners. Develop co-authored technical guidance and design visuals showing how ADA-compliant depaved sites with tree canopy can meet emergency access standards, including EMS vehicle clearance thresholds. This documentation should include sample layouts for tree-shaded play areas and green infrastructure installations that satisfy both accessibility and safety requirements.** **Simultaneously, explore new funding mechanisms—such as dedicated maintenance set-asides in bond measures or interagency memoranda of understanding—that ensure ongoing care for green infrastructure installations.
### ~~**E. Prioritization Tools and Small District Support**~~
~~**Challenge:**~~~~ Most school districts lack tools to match site needs (heat, flooding, etc.) with best-fit strategies, and smaller districts lack capacity to act.~~
~~**Recommendation:**~~~~ Create a decision matrix based on hazard exposure, tie it to land cover data, and make it public. Offer turnkey intervention menus for small districts (fewer than 25 schools), complete with grant writing templates and plug-and-play project designs. In addition, the County should coordinate with the Department of Toxic Substances Control (DTSC) to create a baseline soil risk classification for school properties. This could help districts—especially smaller ones—identify which sites are eligible for shallow interventions without triggering costly remediation, thereby streamlining early-stage planning and proposal development.~~
### **F. Tree Infrastructure Standards**
**Challenge:** Undersized tree wells—such as those measuring 3 by 3 feet—are common on school campuses and limit the growth, health, and longevity of shade trees, thereby undermining long-term canopy, cooling, and climate resilience goals.
**Recommendation:** Encourage the use of larger tree wells (ideally 6 by 6 feet or greater) wherever site conditions allow. Provide design visuals that illustrate how tree well sizing impacts canopy growth, long-term cooling, ecological benefits, and reductions in energy costs—making these interventions both environmentally and financially efficient. Where space is limited, offer alternative strategies—such as linear planters or connected rooting zones—that support healthy tree growth and deliver similar cooling outcomes in constrained conditions.
### ~~**G. Site Classification and Community Engagement**~~
~~**Challenge:**~~~~ partners worry that publishing “most in need” rankings may hurt school trust or funding eligibility.~~
~~**Recommendation:**~~~~ Publish site typologies (e.g., shovel-ready, needs assessment, long-term remediation) without numerical rankings. Use these to coordinate funders and reduce redundancy.~~
### ~~**H. Integration with Watershed Planning**~~
~~**Challenge:**~~~~ School campuses are rarely treated as joint-use assets or stormwater capture hubs.~~
~~**Recommendation:**~~~~ Include schools in SCWP watershed planning. Dedicate funding to pilot “school park” retrofits that serve both water and open space needs in park-poor neighborhoods.~~
-----
### 6.1.5 Private Property Incentives and Constraints
### **A. Commercial and Industrial Parcels**
**Challenge:** Commercial and industrial properties, especially large parking lots, represent high-impact opportunities for depaving. Despite stormwater fee structures that reward impervious surface reduction, most commercial landowners have not pursued depaving retrofits due to cost, lack of technical support, and weak enforcement of LID mandates.
Additionally, many vacant commercial and industrial parcels exhibit high pavement coverage but lack active use, making them ideal early targets for transformation. However, these parcels are not currently prioritized in County programs, and owners lack clear incentives or streamlined pathways to undertake depaving.
**Recommendation 1 – Launch a Turnkey Support Program:**
The County should develop a commercial and industrial depaving initiative that bundles financial, regulatory, and technical assistance into a streamlined offering. This program could include tools such as a fee savings calculator, downloadable design templates, and subsidized engineering support funded through SCWP. Pilot projects should focus on high-heat, high-impact zones—such as large retail parking lots in vulnerable communities.
To lower early adoption barriers, SCWP could fund site-specific parking demand studies and offer a menu of pre-qualified contractors. Discretionary zoning incentives—such as height bonuses or use changes—could be offered to projects that successfully implement depaving retrofits. The Department of Regional Planning (DRP) should be invited to review these recommendations and offer input, particularly on zoning tools and incentive structures. SCWP could explore mechanisms such as tax credit trading and seed a mini-grant fund for early adopters.
**Recommendation 2 – Expand Policy Triggers and Streamline Incentives:**
To broaden uptake, the County should review feasible policy triggers for requiring or encouraging depaving assessments—such as during discretionary planning processes, major site retrofits, or voluntary sustainability certifications. While mandatory parking demand studies at resale or use change may not be appropriate in the current economic climate, voluntary studies could still be encouraged through financial or permitting incentives.
The County should also clarify and improve participation in the underutilized Measure W tax credit trading mechanism. Many landowners remain unaware of its existence or face barriers to participation. Educational outreach, simplified application processes, and targeted support for smaller parcels could improve uptake. Where appropriate, depaving retrofits should be eligible for expedited permitting and discretionary land use benefits, including height bonuses, reduced setback requirements, or flexible parking minimums.
#### **Recommendation 3 – Launch a Vacant Parcel Pilot Program**
The County should establish a pilot initiative specifically focused on vacant commercial and industrial parcels with high pavement intensity. These sites represent "low-hanging fruit" where depaving can deliver immediate stormwater, heat mitigation, and biodiversity benefits without disrupting existing operations.
The program should include pre-approved design templates, streamlined permitting pathways, and targeted financial incentives—such as one-time grants or reductions in stormwater fees—to motivate owners. Mapping and publicly sharing high-priority vacant parcels can also attract private, non-profit, or developer partnerships to accelerate transformation efforts.
In parallel, the County should coordinate with the Department of Regional Planning (DRP) and SCWP watershed coordinators to align these pilots with broader canopy and stormwater targets, ensuring that vacant parcel transformations contribute to regional climate and equity goals.
### **B. Residential Program**
**Challenge:** Private residential parcels are often excluded from climate adaptation planning despite offering cumulative depaving benefits across neighborhoods. Most homeowners lack resources or incentives to remove pavement, and permitting complexity deters participation.
**Recommendation:** The County should identify a lead department or interagency team to launch a residential depaving program as part of the 5-year pilot detailed in Section VII. This program should include both DIY and DIY+ tracks. The DIY track would offer an illustrated guidebook, a permitting flowchart, and coordination with L.A. County Library’s Tool Lending Library. The DIY+ track would support subsidized contractor services for material removal, hauling, and vegetation installation.
SCWP Education Grants could be leveraged to fund early outreach, supported by NGOs and watershed coordinators who provide multilingual and culturally tailored assistance. All interested residents would be eligible to participate. Pre-screening using heat, flooding, and income vulnerability maps would be used to prioritize resources and tailor interventions where they can have the greatest climate and equity impact.
To maximize reach, the County should integrate residential depaving materials into existing communication infrastructure, such as annual tax mailers or resource packets. NGOs and watershed coordinators should be resourced to develop culturally tailored educational materials and engage communities through door-to-door outreach, tree planting collaborations, and neighborhood workshops.
-----
### 6.1.6 Maintenance, Enforcement, and Legal Barriers
### **A. Operations & Maintenance**
**Challenge:** Across County departments, long-term maintenance of newly depaved and greened areas remains a major challenge. Funding constraints, staff limitations, and institutional uncertainty often result in deferred care or dead trees.
**Recommendation:** Include maintenance planning as a required component of all depaving projects funded through SCWP and Measure A. Fund maintenance endowments or explore options for community-based contracts that align with Adopt-a-Tree or similar programs. O\&M pilots should include shared maintenance agreements between LASAN, LAUSD, and nonprofit partners, especially on school campuses where jurisdictional complexity has historically delayed project execution. Public Works and LASAN should integrate irrigation retrofits or passive water capture into planting designs.
### **B. ADA Compliance and Emergency Access**
**Challenge:** In the public right-of-way, concerns around ADA compliance and emergency access can inhibit tree planting or result in excessive repaving—often due to overly cautious or inconsistent interpretations of standards.
**Recommendation:** Design updates should clarify minimum ADA-compliant sidewalk widths to avoid unnecessary over-paving during repairs. Provide site visuals and interagency design guidance to help departments balance accessibility, emergency standards, and greening goals.
### **C. Soil Contamination Risk**
**Challenge:** Many high-priority depaving sites—including schools but many others as well—lack clear soil quality documentation or are covered by capping requirements, creating regulatory hurdles and potential liability.
**Recommendation:** Conduct a County-wide audit of soil risk thresholds under current MS4, DTSC, and CEQA standards. The County should partner with DTSC to develop a shared exposure classification matrix and screening checklist for project applicants, with an internal version for agency use and a public-facing version to support NGOs and community partners. Prioritize partnerships with DTSC and LAUSD to create a publicly accessible review and exemption process for shallow interventions.
The County should explore, in coordination with County Counsel, potential pathways for clarifying funding eligibility and indemnity requirements when public funds are used on private parcels. This may include reviewing legal frameworks already applied in Measure W projects. If feasible, future steps could also assess whether enabling legislation is needed to support shallow interventions on school and residential properties where contamination risks are low.
-----
### 6.1.7 Interdepartmental Coordination and Governance
### **A. Agency Roles: Alignment & Accountability**
**Challenge: **Tree planting, pavement removal, stormwater planning, and environmental health are managed by different County departments, resulting in siloed operations and misalignment in project design, grant applications, and community engagement. While the newly created *Depave + Shade Taskforce* provides a promising venue for interdepartmental coordination, the County still lacks formal mechanisms for shared accountability, implementation tracking, and alignment of capital projects with depaving priorities. Past efforts, such as interdepartmental meetings hosted by PLACE, have struggled to maintain authority and consistent follow-through.
**Recommendation:** The County should leverage the newly established *Depave + Shade Taskforce* housed within the PLACE program (?)—co-chaired by \_\_\_\_\_ —as the central venue for interdepartmental coordination, policy alignment, and process streamlining. To strengthen accountability, each participating agency should designate a standing appointee with decision-making authority and oversight of depaving-related capital improvements.
This taskforce should convene quarterly and be resourced to facilitate implementation tracking, GIS updates, and pilot evaluation through the CSO. PLACE should also host annual design charrettes focused on barrier-solving and workflow alignment, rather than retrospective reporting.
To support consistent implementation, County departments should co-develop standard operating procedures (SOPs) and decision flow maps for incorporating pavement removal into capital projects. In parallel, Public Works and CFMP should jointly maintain a decision tree that clarifies which retrofit types—such as tree wells or bioswales—are appropriate for given parcel conditions. This effort should be complemented by an audit of tree clearance requirements to ensure they balance safety with long-term canopy expansion goals.
The existing Sustainability Leads Council should reinforce this effort by reviewing departmental RFPs at their quarterly meetings, flagging instances where pavement removal is not considered, and recommending revisions to better align projects with County depaving goals.
-----
### 6.1.8 Pilot Program and Toolkit Deployment
### **A. Program Design**
**Challenge:** The CFMP was approved by the Board of Supervisors and includes an action part for a pilot depaving program, but lacks implementation structure or funding.
**Recommendation:** Launch a 5-year Countywide Depave Pilot Program to test and refine approaches across five site typologies. To support startup costs and coordination, the County should explore opportunities to leverage  Measure W funding While specific budgets will need refinement, a hypothetical allocation might include:
  - $15M – Commercial parking lots  
  - $10M – Residential sites (including DIY and DIY+)  
  - $15M – County-owned or public land (e.g., rights-of-way, schools, parks, campuses)  
  - $5M – Program management  
  - $5M – Technical assistance and monitoring
Program management should reside with the Chief Sustainability Office or a designated coordinating agency, with a steering committee including representatives from PLACE, SCWP, and Regional Planning. At least one pilot project should be funded in each of the following divisions: Public Works (rights-of-way), Urban Forestry (tree well cutouts), and County Properties (e.g., hospitals, schools, civic centers, parks). These early pilots should be used to surface department-specific barriers, permitting challenges, and operations and maintenance constraints. 
To maximize public benefit and workforce development, the program should also prioritize partnerships with local high schools, colleges, and universities—particularly planning, architecture, landscape architecture, construction management, and sustainability programs. Pilots should offer internship-based opportunities for students to collaborate with County agencies and the Depave + Shade Taskforce on long-term visioning for the five typologies.
For residential and small commercial sites, logistics, contractor coordination, and community engagement should be managed by NGOs or pre-qualified consultants.
### ~~**B. Design Toolkit**~~
~~**Finding:**~~~~ There is no consolidated technical resource for County departments, consultants, or applicants seeking to implement depaving solutions.~~
~~**Recommendation:**~~~~ Develop a “Depaving Adaptation Toolkit” that includes site selection guidance, planting and bioswale specs, standard sidewalk and curb cut designs, and a decision tree based on land use type and community vulnerability. Include design visuals that demonstrate tree well dimensions, root zone allowances, and ADA integration. ### 6.1.10 Pilot Projects to Advance depaving strategies
To help operationalize the depaving strategies outlined in this report, we propose a set of pilot projects designed to demonstrate, refine, and scale depaving interventions. These pilots are organized into three tiers, from most readily implementable to most ambitious, to help agencies and municipalities select context-appropriate actions.

These simple, highly visible interventions offer immediate pedestrian safety improvements, support Vision Zero goals, and provide an accessible entry point for cities new to depaving.~~
