# SCWP-specific Material

# Material for Outreach to Specific Agencies
## **5.4 SCWP Incentives**
### 5.4.1 SCWP Tax Credit Analy<comment_start id=kix.hixyzo3e10w>sis<comment_end id=kix.hixyzo3e10w>
### **5.4.1.1 SCWP Tax Incentives and Credits: Background and Key Points**
Established in 2018 through Measure W, Los Angeles County's Safe, Clean Water Program (SCWP) levies a parcel tax of 2.5 cents per square foot on impermeable surfaces to fund stormwater projects. This program generates between $285 million and $300 million annually. To incentivize private property owners to contribute to stormwater management, the SCWP features a Tax Credit Program. This allows owners who install approved stormwater improvements, such as rain gardens or permeable pavements, to receive credits toward their tax liability. It is possible to achieve a full 100% tax waiver by accumulating credits for water quality, water supply, and community benefits. Additionally, a credit trading system permits tax-exempt entities like non-profits to earn and sell Tax Credit Trading Units (TCTUs) to other taxpayers. However, the core issue is whether these incentives are substantial enough to offset the high costs of retrofitting properties.
### **5.4.1.2 The Commercial Scenario**
For commercial properties, the financial incentive provided by the SCWP tax credit is minimal compared to the cost of implementation. Consider a 20,000-square-foot commercial lot where 2,000 square feet of pavement is converted to green infrastructure, a project that could cost approximately $200,000. The yearly tax savings from this reduction would only be $50. Over a 30-year span, this amounts to just $1,500. Even in the most favorable scenario, where the project earns a 100% tax credit for the entire property, the annual savings would be $500, or $15,000 over 30 years, covering only about 7.5% of the initial investment.
This analysis reveals that the tax credit alone does not present a compelling financial case for commercial property owners to undertake such projects. The payback period extends far beyond a typical investment horizon, indicating that the tax rate of 2.5 cents per square foot is too low to spur significant private investment without other motivating factors. These could include corporate sustainability goals, addressing on-site flooding issues, or leveraging additional grants.
### **5.4.1.3 The Residential Scenario**
The financial disparity is even more pronounced in the residential sector. A homeowner with a 6,000-square-foot property might spend around $60,000 to professionally remove 600 square feet of pavement and install landscaping. The resulting annual tax saving would be a mere $15. This equates to $450 over 30 years, an insignificant return on the substantial upfront cost.
If the project were comprehensive enough to warrant a full tax credit on the property's impervious areas, the annual savings might increase to about $135, totaling $4,050 over three decades. This is still less than 10% of the project's cost. Such a small return is unlikely to motivate the average homeowner to invest in a major depaving project. This is in sharp contrast to cities like Philadelphia, which have implemented higher stormwater fees, thereby creating a more potent financial stimulus for private green infrastructure initiatives.
### **5.4.1.4 Industrial and Government Scenarios**
Industrial sites, characterized by large impermeable surfaces, often face significant SCWP tax bills. For a five-acre property, the annual tax could exceed $5,400. While eliminating this tax could save over $160,000 in 30 years, this amount is often negligible compared to the multi-million dollar cost of a full-scale retrofit. Consequently, industrial property owners are typically more motivated by the need to comply with stormwater discharge regulations than by the tax credit itself.
Government and non-profit properties are generally exempt from the SCWP tax, meaning they do not have a direct financial incentive to reduce their impervious surfaces. They can, however, participate in the Credit Trading Program by installing stormwater projects and selling the generated credits. The effectiveness of this market is currently limited by the low tax rate, which dampens demand from potential buyers. Public agencies are more inclined to undertake green infrastructure projects using direct funding from the SCWP, setting a precedent for private entities.
#### **5.4.1.5 Monitoring **
Another observation is the data and credit mechanism within SCWP could be refined. Currently, the SCWP tax assessment uses aerial land cover data to calculate total impervious area per parcel, but it does not differentiate between types of impervious surface (roof vs. pavement) in the billing. For credit purposes, what matters is the *total* volume or area of runoff reduced. But from a program evaluation standpoint, it would be useful to know how the impervious reduction was achieved – e.g. did the owner remove parking asphalt or install a green roof? These interventions have different co-benefits and maintenance needs. Going forward, the County may need higher-resolution tracking of interventions to ensure credits are quantified and verified accurately. For example, a green roof might retain X inches of rain, whereas removing pavement opens soil for infiltration – the models to calculate water quality benefit might differ. A more granular accounting could also potentially enable targeted incentives (perhaps a higher credit for certain beneficial practices if warranted). Currently, the analytics are coarse, which is fine for administering a flat tax, but refining the monitoring and credit verification will make the incentive program more effective and trustworthy.

## **Appendix B: Preliminary Target Calculation**
#### **Preliminary Target calculation su<comment_start id=kix.5nm0e2ql31ay>mmary<comment_end id=kix.5nm0e2ql31ay>**
While the above mentioned target-setting framework is being developed and implemented, we use a simpler method to calculate a preliminary set of proposed targets for the Our County Plan developed concomitantly with this assessment. For a detailed description of the method for calculating these depaving targets, see chapter 7.3.4. In brief,  we screened every tax parcel in Los Angeles  County, calculating its current landscaped share and estimating “non-core” pavement (non‑road, non-building hardscape minus required parking). Commercial parcels were assessed against a 10 % landscape goal, residential parcels against 20% of non-building area goal. Only parcels falling short of the goal and possessing sufficient excess pavement received a removal target. School campuses were handled separately: targets for LAUSD reported in the Los Angeles Unified School District Green Schoolyards for All Plan were extrapolated county‑wide to account for non‑LAUSD sites. All pavement on vacant parcels within the overlapping hotspot tiers discussed in chapter 2.6.2.4 is also included, as well as the 10% of non-core pavement in ROW in the hotspots, and 1% of the industrial pavement in the hotspots.
**Aggregate Result**
|  |  |
| :- | :- |
| **Target Category** | **Acres depaving target** |
| Residential Landscape Requirement | 571 |
| Commercial Landscape Requirement | 1018 |
| 1% of Industrial Pavement in 50th percentile Hotspots | 62 |
| Vacant parcels in hotspots | 7034 |
| 10% of ROW non-core pavement in hotspots | 421 |
| School extrapolation | 641 |
|  |  |
| **Total** | **9747** |

Table. 5.4
This headline figure provides a concrete, spatially targeted objective for county‑wide greening, heat and flood mitigation initiatives.

## **District 2 key locations**
### Opportunity Sites District \#2
### \[image\]
##### **Figure 8.1: District 2 Sites**
Supervisorial District 2 fell into numerous categories for prioritization from flooding risk to low tree cover and high enviroscreen rating. Because district 2 communities regularly occurred on all the weightings, we went a step further in this area, as an example of what could done in each area and identify Specific Possible County owned land: parks, libraries, schools or ROW Sites in Florence-Firestone/Willowbrook/Athens/Westmont (See depaving strategies section for detailed maps of these site intervention examples) 
## **Representative Sites**
Representative sites were chosen from around the county for the purposes of obtaining pricing from contractors. Some of these sites are shown below:
## **ROW**
### **Compton Ave**
Compton Avenue has a fairly standard sidewalk width but there are still many places where a 4' wide planting area could be added.
\[image\]\[image\]
##### **Figure 8.2: Compton Ave**
### **Central Ave**
Central Avenue has two wide sidewalks \>12'. Existing planting areas occur intermittently and are more than 8' wide in some places. Red boxes indicate just some areas new planters could be added within the existing sidewalk. Orange boxes indicate unused areas where significant pavement could be removed within the right of way, but would require more coordination. Additionally, creative solutions, like planting behind bus stops, could provide needed shade while not impacting visibility and circulation (lower right image).\[image\]\[image\]
\[image\]
##### **Figure 8.3, 8.4, 8.5: Central Ave depaving strategies**
### **Median Planters**
**\[image\]**
##### **Figure 8.6: Median Planters**
Florence has segments with a beautiful median, but then other segments with marked-off, un-drivable, but still paved median. These are great opportunities to continue the planted median\! Additional places to consider are frequent walking corridors, such as this bridge over the rail line in Florence Neighborhood that connects the western community to the library on Graham.
## **Transit Parking Lot**
**\[image\]\[image\]**
##### **Figure 8.7: Transit Parking**
Angled parking, like this station in Florence Neighborhood, affords a huge potential for this and many other parking lots to depave and increase canopy cover reducing heat island.
## **School Parking Lot**
**Walnut Park Elementary**
\[image\]\[image\]
##### **Figure 8.8, 8.9: Walnut Park Elementary**
Many parking lots like this one have areas that are striped to define drive aisles in nonlinear conditions. These are simple, unused places to depave. Additionally a 3-5' wide planter could be added between parking aisles to increase canopy without losing parking.
## **Industrial Parking Lot**
**\[image\]**
##### **Figure 8.10: Industrial Parking Lot**
This is a real example from south Los Angeles, but applies to many locations. Many parking lots have angled parking to increase efficiency. This approach leaves unused space between lanes that can be harnessed for shade tree planting without losing parking space.  Many cars pull up to the front line, but if there was a planter and a curb stop, there is plenty of unused space behind them (as indicated by the white box).
### Cut Sheets
Cut sheets were developed for depaving strategies to share with contractors for pricing purposes. The two cut sheets below are representative.
\[image\]\[image\]
##### **Figures 8.11, 8.12: Sample Cut Sheets**
### Cost Analysis Details
Pricing estimate spreadsheets were received similar to the image below:
\[image\]
##### **Table X: Contractor Submitted Cost Estimate**
Databases were obtained from L.A. County. A screenshot is below:
\[image\]
##### **Table X: L.A. County Pricing Data**
The costs from the contractors and databases were utilized in creating cost estimates for typologies. Costs were summarized in dollars per square foot for the approximate project area size listed. If ten 100 square foot project sites were included in a single project, the cost would be closer to a 1,000 square foot project. 
The cost estimates compiled and analyzed show a cost of approximately $100 to $170 per square foot for the six project types which have been calculated, which include: wide sidewalk, narrow sidewalk/street, bus stop, bike lane buffer, paved median w/curb and streets with turn lane and driveable medians. The costs were estimated by aggregating the subtasks involved in implementing the typology. Some of the costs that made some estimates more expensive include the cost of traffic control on larger streets, and also the cost of connecting to a water meter and permanent irrigation. Costs are lower on side streets or if plantings can be done that do not require permanent irrigation. The cost for temporary irrigation for establishment and the cost for ongoing maintenance have not been factored in at this point. The cost for utility location is highly dependent on the size of the street and the complexity of the utilities present. The cost for potentially encountering contaminated soil has also not yet been considered.
The contractors who have submitted prices have said that they are interested to work on these projects and they are willing to submit additional bids when the projects are more detailed and the designs are ready.  A screenshot of the table submitted by a contractor  is below.
\[image\]
##### **Figure X: Preliminary Cost Estimates per Square Foot**
Valuable information was obtained by reviewing the Tree Planting Cost Benefit Analysis by the non-profit Tree People organization. It’s important to factor in both maintenance and administrative costs when evaluating the cost of these projects.
\[image\]
##### **Figure 8.13: Tree People Tree Planting Costs**
\[image\]
##### **Figure X: Tree People Tree Planting Costs**
One strategy is the installation of sidewalk planters or bioswales. An example is shown below
\[image\]
##### **Figure 8.14: Bioswale Cross Section**
Another example is installation of tree wells as is shown below:
\[image\]

              - UCLA project, “community-focused data science lab,” civil and environmental engineering-led program, student-led
                  - Want to interact on hands-on projects with CBOs and people working in field 
                  - Committed to compiling databases, dashboards and so on
                  - As brainstorming available datasets, considered green schools america, SCWP data for the stormwater piece, and led to discovery of DepaveLA
                  - Dashboard from school greening that could combine existing data sets and others that have yet to be developed 
                      - To ID opportunities, prioritize, and list those conditions
              - Jason Casanova (JC, “Cass”) added a note about geography: what’s missing is comparison of schools across the whole district, which is where DepaveLA comes in    
  - DP: happy that SCWP prioritizing schools
      - Confirming the data viewer visible alongside report when released in January
          - Some data will be available for download in static map format if there is interest
          - Integrating into another platform is something that would need to be coordinated with Hyphae rather than ARLA
  - BB: we have different data sets that will be released to public vs what can be shared privately
      - Broached the privacy concerns 
      - But happy to share with partners
      - Re: the student participation, a student class is not perhaps best suited to developing a dashboard, nor do we need more dashboards, is BB’s take
          - Prefer to teach students to work on an existing dataset
          - We have a ranked system by need already 
              - Recommendation to look at drainage and slope e.g. or decide collectively with students what they want to look at 
              - Do site-specific work that refers back to their classwork: topographical, lidar, calcs re: civil engineering etc…
              - Can send a section of the report, e.g. schools analysis 
              - Can include that as an accompaniment to an embargo sent to ARLA comms teams
  - JC: What’s missing in current data on schools, GSA e.g., is limited to play area – they want overall school snapshot 
      - BB: we could give them the school list to do test-fit layouts for parking lots
          - We could participate and do charettes with them 
          - Can share with design and setback skill development
  - KK: 
      - Shared screen: listing avail datasets
          - Shows where the gaps are in school greening and to advance school greening   
          - Aware of the limitations of students, but to get them on the data synthesis task would be a good start to advance the goal and determine what is helpful data   
              - Discussion about “community-focused data science lab,” is it an advanced class, is it GIS or civil engineering focused?
  - DP: Depava analysis looks at overlapping needs
      - But analysis is not granular enough to ID which angle of this schoolyard eg has the downflow of water
          - Could imagine a project that would do more detailed analysis of top-ranking schools 
          - But LAUSD has its own data, ranking prioritization etc
              - And there is an advantage to synthesize and cross-reference those data sets against each other
              - We can share static info and you can keep us posted as scope evolves and gets more defined
              - BB offered to participate, send EF and EO to do an intro class e.g.
  - BB: re: Brett Saunders PRIMO data set, you can measure datasets against each other 
      - Work with DF to get topo maps for each site e.g. could be fun project 
          - “Produce two-foot contour maps for each school” e.g. 
          - We could also scan in person as an activity 
          - As well as thinking about future grants to pursue with CWS
      - Re: watershed plans, BB suggested we collaborate on improving watershed plans with this data 
          - DP: watershed plans not to be updated for another five years 
          - “But we feel strongly about feeding it to program in other ways.”
              - Would love to present to coordinators
              - Possible? KK: Yes, can arrange for next year (January), after report release 
                  - JC: two new watershed coordinators being onboarded, could be a good opportunity for discussion        
Action items
- [ ] DP-Hyphae: send some info fro report timed for report release in January  
## **Nov 19, 2025**
## **ARLA x Hyphae re: Data Viewer Scope & Consortium Concept**
<comment_start id=kix.hg0sbawpec7t>Summary<comment_end id=kix.hg0sbawpec7t>:
Re: *Data Viewer*
  - The bulk of the meeting was spent discussing scoping of data viewer.
      - Main disconnect that emerged was between a typical project page linking to a pdf
      - Vs a dynamic interactive web experience of the report
          - The concern was that ARLA had been conceiving of the project as the former
          - And questioned whether *all* rather than *some* of the maps and figures needed this level of sophisticated data viewing
          - ARLA subsequently sent a revised scope linked [here](https://docs.google.com/spreadsheets/d/1AtporXTHeOz7rw5USgkUQgXsrOdcNk7B3F_suv3bxLI/edit?gid=802889496#gid=802889496) agreeing to a pared-down budget.
          - JB had explained that budget constraints did not allow to fund a more substantial data viewer, despite how visually appealing the work we’ve done is
Re: *Consortium*
  - ARLA’s preference was for the report to come out and generate interest from partners and agencies to whom we (Hyphae x ARLA) would lend out support
  - But to scope out and fund a consortium would be premature, despite the expectation that the report will generate a lot of interest
  - Leading with a fully fleshed-out consortium idea, ARLA held, might be perceived as 
      - a) a little presumptuous and 
      - b) too controlling of the data
  - The recommendation was to first socialize the data, and then build on that momentum, combined with ARLA’s comms efforts, to implement the data organically
  - BB’s retort was that we at Hyphae need to start scoping out these grant applications now so we have less time to wait for that process to unfurl over the long term
      - We don’t want to be reliant on ARLA for money
      - The idea of the consortium is just to agree on what we are trying to do collectively and then set out to raise the funds to execute 
Meeting closed with:
  - A decision to table the conversation about the consortium to focus on the more immediate priorities:
      - Finalizing the licensing agreement w/ Spherical
          - <comment_start id=kix.lje1uf8x6xxv>In a follow-up email (11/20/25), JB wrote that <comment_end id=kix.lje1uf8x6xxv>
              - a licensing agreement is unnecessary because ownership and contractual relationships are already clearly established. 
              - ARLA (as a sponsored project of RPA) and RPA own the Field Kit and have already funded the depave analysis. 
              - Since Spherical is working under a services contract to build and maintain the Field Kit, sharing the depave work product (the hotspots data layer) with Spherical is simply an extension of ARLA/RPA integrating their own funded work into their own tool rather than a transfer requiring separate licensing. 
              - Hyphae just needs to supply the hotspot data asap  
      - And completing the report on time
      - Need to schedule a call for a more expansive explanation of what the comms and outreach effort entails  
CU Tasks:
- [ ] [Send hotspot data to Spherical ](https://app.clickup.com/t/86b7jj2rn)
- [x] [Schedule a Hyphae x ARLA call for more in-depth comms explanation](https://app.clickup.com/t/86b708knh)
        
## **Nov 18, 2025|**
**ARLA x Hyphae Depave check Ins**
<comment_start id=kix.pwgqkhmmwbsk>Agenda:<comment_end id=kix.pwgqkhmmwbsk>
  - Licensing agreement (20 min)
  - If time, SOW and budget discussion 
Notes:
  - David. Brendan (Spherical) and Jen went through the draft agreement, have some suggestions
  - Draft agreement is very similar to a typical commercial license and ARLA hopes to loosen some of it to reflect how we typically work with the Field Kit
  - David will walk through suggestions:
      - Spherical is ARLA’s contractor, developing the platform (Field Kit) that is owned by RPA (ARLA’s fiscal sponsor). 
      - Wanting to clarify Spherical is acting on ARLA’s behalf in developing the Field Kit which is owned by ARLA as a nonprofit entity, not commercial entity
      - Tried to suggest clarifications in the agreement with that in mind 
      - ARLA is a partner + funder of the research, different relationship than a commercial licensee that may come along in future 
      - Went line by line through contract edits (MP to self: return to transcript for summary)
  - No intention by ARLA or Spherical to use the depave data outside of the Field Kit 
  - ARLA is planning to share the link to the depave data viewer site for uses - accessed via creative commons license 
  - MP to self: Return to segment on indemnification (not originally included, but likely that RPA will want to include that)
  - Next steps: Spherical to send edits back to us, Hyphae, we send to attorney, then send back to RPA
  - BB section (revisit) about clarifying original intent 
  - “Data layer” commitment from David?
  -  Maps, etc will be downloadable as exhibits 
  - Other archiving of data can be handled separately - this agreement is explicitly for displaying data on the Field Kit 
  - Questions of how to deliver the data: stylized or not stylized
  - Re: RPA coordination, question of timing
      - Holiday season complicates that timing, but will do our best 
  - Setting the stage for budget discussion
      - DB: DP and I put comments
          - Mismatch between expectations, what line items mean, explain what appears as duplicative, not on     
Next steps:
- [x] JB to send the copy of agreement edited by Spherical  
## **Nov 11, 2025 | [ARLA x Hyphae Depave Check Ins ](https://www.google.com/calendar/event?eid=MmRrNmFiMWUwY203aXZiYmc4cGJkbDl0aTVfMjAyNTExMTFUMjMwMDAwWiBtYXhAaHlwaGFlLm5ldA)**
Attendees: [Daniel Fleischer](mailto:fletch@hyphae.net) [Deborah Bloome](mailto:dbloome@acceleratela.org) [](mailto:dprovo@accelerateresiliencela.org) [Brent Bucknum](mailto:brent@hyphae.net) [Maxwell Pingeon](mailto:max@hyphae.net) [Jennifer Bravo](mailto:jbravo@acceleratela.org)
Agenda:
  - Demonstration of web viewer + q\&a (15 min)
  - Discussion of scopes (15)  
 
Notes
  - BB presenting data viewer
Re: scoping
  - DP: from Hyphae standpoint, would this be accessible from Hyphae’s website 
      - Bec it will be clickable from ARLA website
      - Would not want to have user routed through Hyphae site? 
          - Where does homepage live?
          - BB: this would be a depaveLA site that would link from both Hyphae and ARLA page
          - DP: what we want is the data viewer and not the hosting capability of other pages
          - JB: wants to create a page for this project as we have for all our projects 
              - Makes sense to have a page on Hyphae web site, we just want to have to link through Hyphae page 
              - JB not expecting to create a whole new website – only a data viewer that lives on a server 
                  - They did not expect that we were creating a whole new web site… 
              - DP: Ok that these pages exist, just not what ARLA wants to scope
              - JB: hopeful that data viewer capacity can be isolated as a specific link
Re: capacity/function 
  - DB: So can you locate needs based on community: e.g. pacoima tree canopy
      - Put in the address?
      - Yes, address filtered is an option, can be a wider area
      - DB: yes a zip code for example would be good 
      - BB: presently can filter by district and community area 
  - DB: scale question
      - DF: currently each side if hex is 70 meters so 140 meters (450 ft)   
      - BB: Typically not a house but a block 
      - DB to JB and DP: How small scale do we show in Field Kit? Don’t know off hand.
  - DP: can this show core v. non-core 
      - DF: I circulated a data dictionary: can choose attributes as from a menu 
          - parking, land cover, amt of pavement considered non-core 
      - DB: Concern that the residences could appear as “non-core” pavement 
          - DF: Even if we make caveats in data viewer
          - DF recommends we include everything but non-core, for simplicity’s sake also 
          - DB: good, aligned 
Re: budget
  - BB: discussion of budget 
      - Setting aside costs for going for grants together and coordination in separate scope 
      - $ for live interaction usability of reports 
          - Page esp set aside for schools 
      - Bug testing and serving the data over the course of a year 
          - ARLA may not want to pay for more than one year (current scope is for 2 years)
      - Will send out budget to have ARLA/RPA mark up          
Action items
- [ ]

Agenda
  - Discuss data sharing agreement

<comment_start id=kix.1javdq1022y8>Notes<comment_end id=kix.1javdq1022y8>
  - Brent to walk team through data sharing agreement 
      - To give to ARLA and Spherical for Living Infrastructure Toolkit for free 
  - Consulted Mapbox and ESRI as standards for licenses  
  - JB clarified that agreement is ultimately to go through RPA
      - JB will have to refine language before sending up the chain to RPA, but wants to clear with ARLA team first 
  - BB: data set made available through a web feature service 
      - Follows standard geospatial processes
          - “Chain of custody” shows how and when the data is being used 
      - Since data set is new and under revision, added clause that:
          - data can be updated and replaced with new data if/when errors are discovered
  - JB: Spherical listed in the document, so they will have to look at it also 
      - BB: yes, or they can be taken out and referred to as a “subcontractor”
          - JB: not sure RPA can sign an agreement w/ Spherical’s name listed in it
  - DB:
      - Clarification of chain of custody function
      - BB: downloading a chart PDF is creative commons (download button = creative commons)
          - Anyone can do this (charts from report)
          - A more detailed formulaire is triggered by those seeking a formal partnership 
          - DP: re: clause for correcting errors, wants to confirm that partners can rely on the report’s data as somewhat fixed rather than constantly changing 
              - BB: we would address that concern by versioning and referencing errors in methodology and findings in future research    
  - BB: 
      - Don’t want to indemnify against lawsuits because giving an “as-is” product 
      - JB: let RPA add their minimum requirements 
  - JB:
      - Tech question: How is data going to be pulled from portal into FieldKit
          - What level of guarantee of uninterrupted service can we offer is her question
          - How can we ward against discrepancies between two sources of data: field kit vs portal?
              - May be a question for Brendan and David 
              - BB: we are happy to add a guarantee to make sure the pipeline with field kit is smooth 
              - JB: does not need to be exhaustive
  - DB:
      - In case of a partner, academic or other, that wants to know more about methodology, how to handle? 
          - BB: Beyond the methods section, we are happy to be totally transparent.
              - Question of publishing the methods section or other material as academic papers.
  - JB: 
      - Relation of ARLA website to both report and viewer
          - Is it the same as “portal”?
              - BB: yes
          - Both uses: 
              - a) spherical’s field kit, which will be pulling from portal
              - b) portal viewer, link from ARLA website, which is a second use case that needs to be in agreement
                  - Link from ARLA web page will be a “passive” view (covered under creative commons)
                  - More active is Spherical’s “dynamic” pulling of data from portal into the field kit
                      - ARLA website has link to PDF, portal viewer (accessible under creative commons)
                      - ARLA is listed as joint author
                  - This licensing agreement is for Spherical - a separate one would be created for the County (and they can decide if they want to charge them or not)
  - Future evolution of the consortium would have different parameters for sharing data 
      - County and other actors would ideally step up to lead a task force and that would shape the evolution of the consortium
          - Potentially have webinar with follow-up with interested CBO partners
          - Potential mutual aid agreement to determine what each group brings to the consortium 
  - Next steps
      - Need to show this agreement first to Brendan and David from Spherical 
      - And then to RPA
      - BB: have DF look at it 
          - JB: can I MP have relevant people confirm with Hyphae tech team that this data sharing agreement is accurate before sending (re: JB edits) 
              - before sending over to Brendan and David to review async 
          - BB: do we need a technical write-up
              - For Spherical yes but not for RPA
  - Comms training 12/8/2025:
      - Timing of meeting pushed back 30 mins 
      - Goal of training is to stay on message and have a tight narrative         
Action items
- [ ]

    "commentId": "AAABoHxMD6U",
    "headPost": {
      "authorName": "Anonymous",
      "content": "Okay I think I am starting to understand this chart. I think there are a few problems with it. One is that the \"source\" ranking is relative (e.g. \"high\") while the DepaveLA ranking is absolute, and the reader has no easy way to compare. Could you translate the ranking into something relative, like a percentile or top 25% or some other way to help the reader interpret the number and compare it to the source? This would also help with the fact that some of these sources, such as CFMP, look only at UA - I know your column for that one says \"UA only\" but did you actually re-rank UA only and then pull those re-ranked numbers? Either way, a relative ranking would help with that problem. Another problem for legibility is that the source ranking, in addition to being relative, is also merely implied - the narrative says you are comparing against \"high need\" but the table doesn't show the designation. So it might help to have an additional column after the sources that says \"high need\" or whatever term the source used to identify the community. Still another problem is that the comparison is only one-way; it starts from the high need communities in other plans and compares that to Depave ranking. What about communities ranked highly in Depave and how they compare to these sources? I see that none of your \"#1s\" are on here, for example.",
